27 Oct 2026

An SRG/eROSITA Study of Be X-ray binaries in Quiescence

Ajith Balu, Kinjal Roy, Biswajit Paul

Be X-ray binaries (BeXRBs) are mostly transient X-ray binaries with usually a neutron star as the compact object and a Be star as their optical companion. We performed a comprehensive analysis of BeXRBs in quiescence with the extended ROentgen Survey with an Imaging Telescope Array (eROSITA), the soft X-ray instrument on board the Spectrum-Roentgen-Gamma satellite, using its first all-sky survey (eRASS1) data. The transient BeXRBs spend majority of time in their quiescent state, but are often studied during their outbursts. A quiescence study is useful to probe the emission mechanism of BeXRBs at low mass accretion rates. The spectra of BeXRBs detected in the eRASS1 main catalogue could be well fitted with a powerlaw as well as a blackbody model. In the absence of detection of a BeXRB, we report the luminosity upper limit for its position in the sky. The X-ray luminosity for the quiescent BeXRBs detected with eROSITA have $L_{X}\sim10^{33-36}$ erg s$^{-1}$ for both absorbed powerlaw and blackbody models in most cases. For the absorbed blackbody model, the emission seems to come from a fraction of the neutron star's surface. The quiescence luminosity of BeXRBs are used to distinguish between different accretion models at low mass accretion rates, like accretion in the propeller regime or from a cold accretion disc. The detection space of BeXRBs in quiescence for the eRASS1 data is compared against previously reported luminosities across multiple X-ray observatories.

A microscopic analysis of shear acceleration in the relativistic regime

Sophie Aerdker, Xingwei Gong, Martin Lemoine, Frank Rieger

Relativistic shear flows, as encountered in jets of powerful astrophysical sources, offer promising sites of particle acceleration up to the highest energies. This stochastic acceleration process is usually described with Fokker-Planck transport equations, which assume small momentum gains per scattering. The present paper introduces a covariant microscopic description of relativistic shear acceleration, building on the formalism of generalized Fermi acceleration and formulating angular scattering in the locally comoving frame in terms of stochastic differential equations. This allows us to model relativistic shear profiles and to study the transition from gradual to non-gradual shear acceleration, where the particle mean free path becomes comparable to the shear length scale. Assuming an infinite shear layer, linear in the flow four-velocity, we observe that the shear rate peaks where the flow is mildly relativistic, whereas Lorentz aberration suppresses the transverse velocity of particles where the flow is relativistic. Consequently, particles that leave the central layer experience long waiting times with a heavy-tailed, power-law distribution. The momentum evolution becomes subdiffusive and the acceleration time can substantially exceed local estimates. We successfully reproduce the Monte Carlo energy distributions using a two-state continuous-time random walk with Lévy-distributed waiting times. We also show how a momentum-dependent mean-free path can introduce breaks in the spectrum and extend our discussion to finite jet profiles. These effects should be accounted for in models of relativistic jets as sources of high-energy particles.

26 Oct 2026

The biases caused by interpolated table models and their impact on X-ray spectroscopy

Matteo Lucchini, Benjamin Ricketts, Phil Uttley, Daniela Huppenkothen, Fergus Baker, Gregoire Marcel, Guglielmo Mastroserio

Models for X-ray spectroscopy involving complex relativistic or plasma physics are commonly pre-computed on a grid, In this paper, we aim to quantify the potential biases and distortions introduced by the computational error and propagated into parameter inference by interpolating linearly these complex, non-linear models. We analyse the performance of Bayesian sampling algorithms on mock inference problems. Then, we model real observations of an accreting black hole X-ray binary in both the soft and hard states. We find that linear interpolation can a) greatly bias the posterior distribution of model parameters and b) distort the likelihood space to such a degree that posterior distributions become artificially multimodal. This multi-modality causes a noticeable loss in computational efficiency in both sampling and fitting techniques. As a result, exploring a given parameter space until convergence requires an extremely large computational cost. These findings call for improving the computational techniques used when fitting modern X-ray data, for example through better sampling algorithms and/or more sophisticated interpolation schemes

Comparison of Hybrid and Standard Reconstruction Methods on IXPE Astrophysical Data

Riccardo Ferrazzoli, Nicolò Cibrario Stefano Silvestri, Michela Negro, Paolo Soffitta, Sudip Chakraborty, Simone Pagliarella, Steven R. Ehlert

The hybrid reconstruction algorithm developed by Cibrario et al. 2023 combined a convolutional neural network (CNN) with standard moment analysis to improve track reconstruction in IXPE Gas Pixel Detectors. Although validated on laboratory data (Cibrario et al. 2025), the method's astrophysical performance had not been systematically characterized. We present the first comparison of hybrid and standard reconstruction on IXPE point sources, measuring sensitivity gains versus energy. We analyze Her X-1, a bright, polarized accreting pulsar as a benchmark, and soft-state XTE J1701-461, a bright neutron-star low-mass X-ray binary with no detected polarization, to assess residual systematics. From both pipelines' Level-2 event files, we compare normalized Stokes parameters, polarization degree (PD) and angle (PA), and detection significance. For Her X-1, hybrid reconstruction increases significance at all energies, significantly above ~2.83 keV. R = σ_hyb/σ_std differs from unity by 9.3σ in the 2.83-4.00 keV bin and reaches 1.18\pm0.03 at 5.66-8.00 keV (6.2σ). For XTE J1701-461, PD is consistent with zero and R with unity. Fourier analysis reveals excess power in hybrid angle distributions at low energies for modes m = 3, 6, 12 and 15, significant at >7σ relative to the standard method; at 5-8 keV, all modes are consistent with noise in both methods. These CNN-amplified hexagonal-grid artifacts do not affect Stokes parameters because they are orthogonal to polarization modes.The method delivers a statistically significant sensitivity gain for Her X-1, consistent with Cibrario et al. 2025 and equivalent to ~20% more effective area without extra observing time. These results support systematic use of hybrid reconstruction on IXPE astrophysical data.

24 Oct 2026

Fast Outflow Driven from the Magnetically Arrested Disc

Taili Yi, Jia-Wen Li

Fast outflows are commonly observed in accreting black hole systems, yet their launching mechanism remains debated, especially when the gas is highly ionized and radiation line driving becomes inefficient. We investigate magnetically driven outflows from magnetically arrested discs (MADs) using a steady, axisymmetric magnetocentrifugal outflow model. The outflow equations follow the classical trans-magnetosonic MHD framework, while the disc structural scalings and outflow-base conditions are motivated by general relativistic magnetohydrodynamics (GRMHD) simulations. We solve for the trans-magnetosonic outflow solutions along prescribed large-scale poloidal magnetic field lines. The outflows launched from the inner disc are strongly accelerated and the formal Newtonian solutions can enter the mildly relativistic regime. The outflow velocity decreases systematically with increasing footpoint radius, producing a radially stratified outflow in which inner streamlines reach UFO-like velocities while outer streamlines form slower components. We also find that the MAD solutions have a large magnetic lever arm $\tilde{l}=(R_{\rm A}/R)^2$ is typically several tens and can reach $\sim10^2$, which implies that the outflow can carry a large amount of the specific angular momentum of the disc material at its footpoint, and we find that about $55\%$ of the angular-momentum estimated between the outer and inner disc boundaries is removed by the magnetic outflow. The total mass-loss rate in the outflow is about $14\%$ of the disc mass-accretion rate in our fiducial model. These results suggest that the MADs provide a natural environment for producing both fast, radially stratified disc outflow and efficient disc angular-momentum removal. MAD-driven outflow may therefore provide a magnetic origin for ultra-fast absorbers in low-luminosity active galactic nuclei (AGNs) and X-ray binaries (XRBs).

23 Oct 2026

Initial Conditions of Magnetized AGN Accretion Disks from Cloud Capture and Disruption

Philip F. Hopkins

Accretion onto supermassive black holes (BHs) from the ISM can occur via capture of cold gas 'clouds' (GMCs, clumps, or overdense structures). Unlike material in stellar tidal disruptions or X-ray binaries, or classical Bondi accretion, ISM clouds are strongly magnetized (plasma $β\equiv P_{\rm thermal}/P_{\rm magnetic} \ll 1$), with supersonic turbulence, and cool rapidly. We derive analytic scalings for the initial conditions of accretion disks formed by capture/disruption of such clouds, in three regimes: (1) low-mass BHs ($M_{\rm BH} \ll M_{\rm cl}$), where turbulent Bondi-Hoyle or hierarchical 'leapfrog' growth via sub-cloud encounters dominates; (2) comparable-mass encounters ($M_{\rm BH} \sim M_{\rm cl}$); and (3) massive BHs tidally disrupting small clouds ($M_{\rm BH} \gg M_{\rm cl}$). In each, the resulting disk is predicted to be initially toroidally-magnetically-dominated ($|B_φ| \gg |B_{z}|$, $β\ll 1$), geometrically thick ($H/R \sim 0.1-1$), and gravitationally stable ($Q \gtrsim 1$), with super-sonic, trans-Alfvenic turbulence. These conditions differ qualitatively from those assumed in classical accretion/$α$ disk theory, 'chaotic cold accretion', stellar tidal disruption, or Bondi-Hoyle accretion. At least initially, the toroidal field should be sustained by flux-freezing on timescales shorter than Parker or turbulent resistive losses. We examine how cloud mass spectra drive accretion rate distributions, duty cycles, and fueling rates, with integrated BH growth dominated by clouds of mass comparable to the BH. Given known cosmological evolution, we suggest a scenario linking (a) inefficient growth and wandering BHs in dwarf galaxies, (b) hyper-Eddington growth in little red dots, (c) peak quasar activity in massive gas-rich systems at cosmic noon, and (d) lower-luminosity AGN in gas-poor galaxies at late times.

Radio Galaxies as UHECR Sources in Light of Recent Microquasar Results

A. M. Taylor, J. H. Matthews

The recent discovery that microquasars operate as efficient particle accelerators brings back into focus the question as to whether their larger cousins, radio galaxies, also operate as efficient accelerators. We here discuss what we presently know about particle acceleration in radio galaxies, focusing particularly on the nearest such object, Centaurus A.

22 Oct 2026

Prompt Jet Launching and Delayed Thermal Disk in Tidal Disruption Event for Prolonged Activity GRB 220706A

Yuri Sato, Rin Oikawa

GRB 220706A exhibits X-ray flaring activity extending to ~51 days after the trigger, along with a luminous optical thermal component emerging on a timescale of ~10^6 s. Such prolonged central-engine activity could be powered by a collapsar fed by an extended stellar envelope. However, a relativistic jet may struggle to break out of the extended envelope. These challenges motivate a tidal disruption event (TDE), although explaining both the early relativistic jet and the luminous late optical thermal emission within one TDE framework remains difficult. We investigate a TDE scenario in which shocks between stellar debris streams drive the early inflow of some debris, potentially carrying preexisting fossil magnetic flux toward the black hole (BH) and powering an initial relativistic jet via the Blandford-Znajek mechanism. The remaining debris circularizes into an accretion disk, whose thermal emission accounts for the late optical thermal component. For GRB 220706A, the optical timescale, luminosity, and color temperature are consistent with disruption of a blue supergiant (M_*~50M_{sun}, R_*~20~R_{sun}) by a BH of mass M_{BH}~10^5 M_{sun}, assuming that the accretion disk forms on a timescale comparable to the orbital period of the most tightly bound stellar debris. A magnetic field strength of ~10^5 G is required near the BH before disruption to power a jet capable of producing the inferred gamma-ray luminosity. Continued fallback may sustain central-engine activity beyond the observed X-ray flaring episode, making future X-ray monitoring a test of this scenario.

Breaking the symmetry: X-ray polarization from a spreading layer on a tilted weakly magnetized neutron star

Anna Bobrikova, Alexandra Veledina, Juri Poutanen, Vladislav Loktev

When modeling the emission from an X-ray binary system, we often assume that the rotational axis of the compact object is aligned with that of the binary system. While often considered irrelevant for spectroscopic observations, the orientation of the binary system is crucial for polarimetric properties. With the recent advent of X-ray polarimetric observations enabled by the Imaging X-ray Polarimetry Explorer (IXPE), indications of a possible misalignment of the neutron star rotational axis with respect to the binary axis have been reported. Following these indications, we present an analytical model for the polarimetric properties of emission arising from a spreading layer on an inclined weakly magnetized neutron star (WMNS). We discuss the expected polarimetric properties and study the impact of geometrical parameters of the system on the polarization of WMNS emission. We compare our results with those for aligned systems. Finally, we discuss applications of the model to recent and upcoming X-ray polarimetric observations with IXPE and mention the suggestions for further development of the model.

21 Oct 2026

The deepest X-ray view of a kiloparsec scale jet at z=6.1

Fabio A. Meanti, Luca Ighina, Alberto Moretti, Kyra Bettwy, Alessandro Caccianiga, Thomas Connor, Fabrizio Tavecchio, Silvia Belladitta, Emmanuel K. Bempong-Manful, Cristiana Spingola

The mechanism responsible for the high-energy emission observed in kiloparsec-scale jets across cosmic time remains poorly understood. The relativistic jet associated with the z=6.1 AGN PSO J030947.49+271757.31 is the highest-redshift large-scale jet currently resolved in X-rays, providing a unique opportunity to study these structures. We present new Chandra observations, combined with archival data, for a total exposure of 360 ks. The increased exposure allowed us to resolve the extended X-ray emission into two distinct, slightly resolved components and perform spatially resolved spectral analyses. The extended emission has a photon index of Gamma = 2.25 (+0.45, -0.44), steeper than that of the core (Gamma = 1.86 +/- 0.09), and an extended-to-core luminosity ratio of 18%. Simulations using the Chandra image as input show that an AXIS-like mission could increase the detected photon statistics by a factor of approximately 17. We resolve the kiloparsec-scale jet into two main components and constrain its spectral properties. This is the most distant and one of the most X-ray-luminous large-scale jets currently known. Future X-ray missions with arcsecond-scale angular resolution could enable more detailed studies of such systems and reveal a population of fainter high-redshift jets beyond Chandra's reach.

SpinOctopus: A Versatile, User-Friendly, and Extensible Public Code for General-Relativistic Ray-Tracing in axisymmetric spacetime

Shiyang Hu, Dan Li, Chen Deng, Lina Zhang, Wenfu Cao

Relativistic ray-tracing algorithms serve as indispensable numerical tools for investigating gravitational theories and high-energy astrophysics. In this paper, we develop and release SpinOctopus, a versatile relativistic ray-tracing code tailored for arbitrary axisymmetric spacetimes, powered by a Fortran/Python hybrid architecture and OpenMP multi-threading acceleration. This numerical toolkit is capable of simulating timelike orbits and their corresponding light curves, null geodesics, as well as black hole images illuminated by a wide array of phenomenological sources, including geometrically thin and thick accretion disks, geometrically thin tilted disks, relativistic jets, spherically symmetric accretion flows, and point sources. To execute these tasks, users are solely required to specify the covariant metric tensor of the target spacetime and the simulation parameters within a dedicated configuration file. Benefiting from our customized adaptive step-size strategy for geodesic integration, SpinOctopus achieves an impressive processing efficiency of $\sim 800$ rays per second per thread on an Intel Core i7-14700 platform when simulating geometrically thick accretion disk images. Furthermore, the code provides transparent interfaces, allowing users to seamlessly modify the geometric and kinematic properties of the light sources. Additional capabilities, such as simulating Fe K$α$ fluorescent line profiles and geometrically thick tilted accretion disk images, will be introduced in future releases of the framework.

20 Oct 2026

Probing the QPO and spectro-temporal evolution of MAXI J1750-327 with XPoSat during its first detected outburst : Constraints on black-hole mass and spin

Meghamani Haldar, Biki Ram, Shubhrangshu Ghosh, Kishor Chaudhury, Rajat K. Dey, Tamal Sarkar

We present a comprehensive timing and broadband spectral study of the newly discovered black hole low-mass X-ray binary candidate MAXI J1750-327 during its August 2026 outburst, using 11 continuous observations obtained with the XSPECT instrument onboard XPoSat. During epochs 1-2, we detect prominent type C QPO exhibiting a systematic upward frequency drift, accompanied by a prominent low-frequency noise component, with a clear transition from type-C to type-B, with subsequent disappearance of QPOs after epoch 4. Broadband spectral modeling reveals that this timing transition is directly mirrored by a fundamental restructuring of the accretion geometry. The inner disk temperature abruptly increases before settling into a steady range, accompanied by a steep reduction in disk normalization across the transition from epoch 3 to 4. This behavior signifies an active dynamical inward migration of the thin accretion disk toward the innermost stable circular orbit (ISCO). The Comptonized flux declines sharply after the QPO turns off, suggesting a transition from a hard to soft state, through intermediate states. We also observed that the spectro-temporal correlations provide compelling physical support for the Lense-Thirring (LT) precession framework to be the origin of the type-C QPOs. Using a Bayesian framework, we constrain the black hole mass to be $M_{\rm BH}=7.92^{+6.19}_{-3.72} M_{\odot}$ and the dimensionless spin parameter to be $a_*=0.163^{+0.116}_{-0.056}$ by jointly modeling the observed type-C QPO frequencies with the global LT precession model and marginalizing over the uncertainties in distance, inclination, and diskbb normalization.

On the superbursts as photodisintegrators of ashes from the rp-process -- Numerical simulations of superbursts with MESA II

M. Nava-Callejas, S. Goriely, N. Chamel

Context: Superbursts are energetic explosions in the crusts of neutron stars in Low-Mass X-ray Binaries (LMXBs). At temperatures above 4 GK, photodisintegration of heavy rp-process ashes may play a major role alongside $^{12}$C fusion. Aims: We investigate how photodisintegration of rp-process ashes affects superburst properties and the initial $^{12}$C abundance required for ignition. Methods: We simulate superbursts with MESA v.24.08.1. Besides the canonical fuel of $20\%$ $^{12}$C and $80\%$ $^{56}$Fe, we test different $^{12}$C abundances mixed with scaled rp-process ashes. Results: Superbursts can ignite with only $10\%$ $^{12}$C when the remaining $90\%$ consists of rp-process ashes. Their luminosity is comparable to that of the canonical fuel, whereas $10\%$ $^{12}$C mixed with $90\%$ $^{56}$Fe yields lower luminosity. $^{56}$Fe remains the most abundant ash nuclide. Although photodisintegration drives nucleosynthesis, neutron, proton and alpha captures release energy at late times. We also find that $A=64$ nuclides or $^{20}$Ne help reduce the $^{12}$C abundance needed for ignition. Conclusions: The composition of accreted material - not just its $^{12}$C content - substantially affects superburst ignition, luminosity and nucleosynthesis.

19 Oct 2026

Evolution of Massive Main-Sequence Stars in Binary Population Synthesis. II. Consequences for Black Hole High-Mass X-ray Binaries and Binary Black Holes

Adam Brček, Ryosuke Hirai, Ilya Mandel, Jack Yutong Chen

Interactions and mass loss during the main-sequence (MS) phase of massive binary stars can significantly alter their subsequent evolution and the properties of the black holes (BHs) they ultimately produce. We use the rapid binary population synthesis code \textsc{COMPAS}, incorporating a new treatment of MS evolution that self-consistently tracks the convective core under arbitrary mass-loss or mass-gain histories. We explore the properties of black hole high-mass X-ray binaries (BH-HMXBs) and binary black holes (BBHs) formed under this new framework. We find that a more detailed treatment of MS evolution is necessary to reproduce the high BH masses observed in BH-HMXBs, which are otherwise absent under prescriptions that do not take the internal structure of MS stars into account. Matching the observed rates and orbital periods favors case A mass transfer that is more non-conservative than commonly assumed. We further find that mass transfer of helium-enriched material during case A mass transfer is common in massive binaries, with potential consequences for the structure of the accreting star. For the BBH population, the new MS framework alters the chirp-mass and mass-ratio distributions of detectable mergers, and case A mass transfer can make a substantial contribution to the BBHs formed through the stable mass transfer channel. These systems show a strong preference for near-equal mass ratios, broadly consistent with recent constraints from gravitational-wave observations.

The accretion flow and jet of M87: broadband modelling across spatial scales

Eduardo M. Gutiérrez, Santiago del Palacio, Tomás Tobías Mazzei

The nearby radio galaxy M87 is an ideal laboratory for studying accretion flows and relativistic jet emission. The EHT collaboration conducted the most comprehensive multi-wavelength observational campaign of M87, in which radio instruments with resolutions ranging from tens of microarcseconds to about an arcsecond sampled different portions of the outflow. We model these data using a radiatively inefficient accretion flow (RIAF) with winds and a new multi-zone jet model, initially magnetically dominated, in which we solve the transport equation for the non-thermal electrons as they are injected, advected, and cooled along the jet. We then compare each radio flux with the jet emission within an effective aperture set by the angular scale of the corresponding observation. The comparison across spatial scales favours a scenario in which the RIAF, rather than the jet, produces the compact $230~{\rm GHz}$ emission, while the jet dominates the lower-frequency radio, optical/UV and X-ray emission, but cannot produce the observed $γ$-rays. The RIAF connects the Bondi-scale gas supply to an accretion rate at the horizon of $1.1\times 10^{-5}$ of the Eddington rate, consistent with EHT polarimetry, and the two jets carry about a quarter of the accretion power, $1.2\times10^{43}~{\rm erg~s}^{-1}$ each. The framework presented in this work provides a simple way to disentangle the accretion-flow and jet contributions to the emission from nearby radio galaxies.

17 Oct 2026

Tidal Disruption Events in Active Galactic Nuclei: Thermal Radiation and Jets

Nimrod Tripto, Tsvi Piran, Julian Krolik

Over the past decade, dozens of Tidal Disruption Events (TDEs) have been identified, with many more expected from upcoming surveys. A small subset has been observed to launch transient, highly luminous relativistic jets, for which the origin of the requisite magnetic flux has not been identified. A non-negligible fraction of TDEs are expected in Active Galactic Nuclei (AGNs), where a magnetized accretion flow is already present. As shown first by Chan et al. (2019), the collision of a TDE debris stream with this flow can substantially alter both disk and debris dynamics, leading to a possibly Eddington-limited flare. Here, we use the Athena++ radiation magnetohydrodynamic (RMHD) code to simulate the time near peak fallback rate of a TDE in an AGN, studying the debris-disk interaction, the radiation emitted, and the accumulation of magnetic flux near the horizon. In the example simulated, the accretion rate climbs to $\sim 12\,\dot{M}_{ \rm Edd}$, while the escaping solid angle-integrated luminosity approaches $\sim L_{ \rm Edd}$. The photon luminosity is strongly asymmetric, brighter in the hemisphere from which the debris strikes the disk and dimmer in the hemisphere toward which the debris travels. The photon spectrum peaks in the EUV. The polar magnetic flux grows $\sim 33$-fold, enough to raise the Blandford--Znajek luminosity by a factor $\sim 10^3$, reaching a level sufficient to power observed TDE jets. The polar magnetic flux is also asymmetric, likely making any jet similarly asymmetric, but in the opposite sense to the photon flux.

16 Oct 2026

The Dancing Bonfire: An Unusual Persistent X-ray Source in M81 Exhibiting Recurrent Luminous Flares

Jiachang Zhang, Zhiyuan Li, Xiangdong Li, Tong Bao, Ziqian Hua, ShanShan Weng

Short-timescale X-ray variability provides a direct probe of unstable accretion onto compact objects, yet extragalactic X-ray binaries (XRBs) with recurrent flaring remain largely unexplored. Here we present a detailed X-ray study of M81F4 (2CXO~J095524.2+690957), a persistently luminous X-ray source towards M81 that exhibits recurrent short X-ray flares. Based on multi-mission X-ray observations spanning two decades, it is found that M81F4 remains persistently bright at $L_{0.5-8.0\rm~keV}\sim10^{38}-10^{39}\rm~erg~s^{-1}$, while producing flares lasting a few hundred seconds and reaching peak luminosities of a few $10^{39}\rm~erg~s^{-1}$, with an observed mean (minimum) recurrence time of $\sim0.5$ day ($\sim$6 ks). The stacked flare profile shows a rapid rise followed by a slower decay, but no significant hardness evolution. Spectral analysis reveals that both the flare and non-flare states are dominated by a hard power-law continuum, with marginal evidence of highly ionized Fe lines in the non-flare state spectrum. These properties disfavor classical Type I and Type II bursts and have no close analog among known XRBs. We favor a magnetized neutron star accreting from an intermediate mass donor at a persistently high rate with the flares arising from instabilities at the disk-magnetosphere boundary. If confirmed, M81F4 might represent a hidden population of XRBs in which sustained, magnetically-regulated accretion can lead to brief episodes of super-Eddington emission.

A Unified Solution to the Low-Mass X-ray Binary Diversity Puzzle: Saturated Magnetic Braking Coupled with Irradiation-Driven Winds

Zhu-Ling Deng, Xiang-Dong Li, Bo Wang, Philipp Podsiadlowski, Yong Shao, Dong-Dong Liu, Zhan-Wen Han

The standard magnetic braking (MB) model has long failed to explain key properties of low-mass X-ray binaries (LMXBs)---including their accretion rates, the formation of ultracompact binaries, and orbital expansion---casting doubt on binary evolution and gravitational-wave progenitor predictions. Phenomenological modifications exist, but a unified, physically grounded mechanism has been missing. Here we show that the overlooked coupling between magnetic saturation and irradiation-driven winds (IDW) provides this missing framework. Accretion-powered irradiation enhances the donor wind, which in turn amplifies MB exactly where needed. Our Saturated MB + IDW (SMB+IDW) model simultaneously resolves three outstanding discrepancies with no fine-tuning: (1) it reproduces the full observed distribution of accretion rates across persistent and transient LMXBs; (2) it naturally explains ultracompact X-ray binary formation; and (3) it quantitatively matches the orbital period distribution of descendant millisecond pulsar (MSP) binaries---a critical independent test failed by all previous models. Critically, the SMB+IDW model transforms MB from an externally imposed condition into a self-consistently regulated process tied to the accretion history. By unifying LMXB evolution to MSPs in a single physical picture, our work resolves the long-standing diversity puzzle and establishes a physics-based foundation for next-generation population synthesis, gravitational-wave progenitor predictions, and compact binary interpretation across cosmic time.

15 Oct 2026

The transformative power of ALMA2040 for mm-VLBI

The Event Horizon Telescope Collaboration

The Event Horizon Telescope (EHT) has resolved horizon-scale emission in M87* and Sgr A*. Extending these observations to fainter black holes, multiple frequencies, and evolving structures requires greater sensitivity and new observing capabilities. We investigate how ALMA2040 could expand the scientific reach of global millimeter very long baseline interferometry (mm-VLBI), compared with ALMA's Wideband Sensitivity Upgrade (WSU). Phasing a larger ALMA array and enabling simultaneous observations across multiple ALMA bands would improve sensitivity and frequency phase transfer, providing spectral and polarimetric diagnostics of gravity, accretion flows, magnetic fields, and relativistic jets. In the configurations studied, ALMA2040 achieves sub-mJy baseline sensitivity at 230 GHz and improves prospects for faint-source imaging at 345 GHz, opening a path from individual horizon-scale targets toward population studies. Simulated observations of M87* show improved multi-frequency recovery of emission from the compact ring-like region toward the jet base, enabling studies of the accretion-jet connection. For Sgr A*, simulations of interstellar scattering mitigation yield a 27% reduction in the error of the recovered intrinsic source structure relative to the WSU configuration. Beyond black holes, these capabilities benefit geometric-distance measurements, stellar masers, compact transients, and other applications of high-resolution astrophysics. Together with rapid-response capabilities across the VLBI network, ALMA2040 could support a transition from isolated snapshots toward multi-frequency, time-resolved studies of compact sources.

14 Oct 2026

mm-jets in the multi-messenger era

Karri I. I. Koljonen, Thomas D. Russell, Britton Jeter, Lauren Rhodes, Alexandra J. Tetarenko, María Díaz Trigo, Talvikki Hovatta, Pau Bosch-Cabot, Mamta Pandey-Pommier, Alessio Marino, Jakob van den Eijnden, Dieu D. Nguyen, Melania Del Santo, Thomas Maccarone, Anna Y. Q. Ho, Maria C. Baglio, Sera Markoff, the ALMA2040 Transient, Time-Domain Astronomy working group

Jets and outflows operate across the full cosmic mass scale from young stellar objects (YSOs) and X-ray binaries (XRBs) to active galactic nuclei (AGN), gamma-ray bursts (GRBs), and tidal disruption events (TDEs), shaping many time-domain and multi-messenger transients. Key questions concern on how jets are launched, accelerated and collimated; where and how they dissipate energy and accelerate particles; and how they couple to their environments to set electromagnetic counterpart diversity and feedback. Millimeter and sub-millimeter observations uniquely address these questions by tracking the evolving synchrotron turnover for robust calorimetry and Lorentz-factor constraints, providing full-Stokes polarization and Faraday diagnostics close to the jet base, and enabling high-resolution imaging from Galactic ejecta to event-horizon-scale AGN jet bases via global Very Long Baseline Interferometry (VLBI). We describe the jet and outflow science enabled by ALMA2040, a next-generation mm/sub-mm interferometer combining an order-of-magnitude sensitivity gain with rapid-response observing, simultaneous multi-band coverage, full-Stokes polarimetry, and integration with global VLBI networks. These capabilities will enable statistically controlled, trigger-driven samples spanning XRB outbursts, early GRB afterglows, protostellar disk winds and jet launching, AGN jet bases, and jetted TDEs, establishing mm/sub-mm astronomy as a cornerstone of time-domain and multi-messenger jet physics in the 2040s and beyond.

GRB 181201A: Spectropolarimetric Constraints on Prompt Emission and Magnetic Field Structure

Dhruv S. Bal, Nikita S. Khatiya, Philippe Laurent, Diego Götz, Dieter H. Hartmann, Ramandeep Gill

Gamma-ray bursts (GRBs) have served as testbeds for high-energy astrophysical phenomena since their discovery over 50 years ago. GRB detections reveal diverse properties across events, complicating efforts to constrain their emission mechanisms, magnetic fields, jet structures, and compositions. Spectropolarimetry is a powerful tool utilized to disentangle these properties. INTEGRAL/IBIS has previously detected three polarized GRBs 041219A ($65\pm 26$%), 061122 ($>$33%) and 140206A ($>$28%). We present the time-integrated and time-resolved analyses of the fourth-polarized GRB 181201A. This GRB exhibits high polarization ($81.5^{+17.2}_{-15.7}$%) over three time bins with the highest signal-to-noise ratio, and the polarization angle (PA) shows a modest temporal evolution. The photon index does not cross the synchrotron line-of-death and shows a hard-to-soft evolution. A high degree of polarization (PD) tends to disfavor a photospheric emission model, as previous studies indicate that this model yields PD $<$ 20%. A modest evolution in the PA points towards a non-axisymmetric jet with a multi-patch emission. Our results indicate that under standard synchrotron conditions, the magnetic field is coherent but lacks global structure. Moreover, the field has multiple patches, but only one or a few dominate due to high degrees of polarization, which overlap with the intrinsic maximum of synchrotron emission within 3$σ$ errors. The high PD is also indicative of the Compton Drag scenario, but only under certain conditions, such as viewing the relativistic jet near its edge. However, given our error bars and the scope for improvement in our pipeline, we cannot strongly favor either scenario; both may remain viable.

LHAASO Constraints on New-Source Cosmic Rays:Abundance Scenarios and Injection Spectral Indices

Xu-Lin Dong, Pei-Pei Zhang, Qi-Ling Chen, Shu-Wang Cui, Yi-Qing Guo

Recent LHAASO observations indicate that microquasars and massive star clusters contribute to Galactic cosmic rays, and likely also to the pre-knee spectral hardening. Their markedly different astrophysical environments, compared with supernova remnants (SNRs), may imprint distinct cosmic-ray abundances. We investigate the spectra and composition of new-source cosmic rays under two abundance scenarios: Case-1, the SNR-like composition with enhanced heavy nuclei, and Case-2, the surface abundances of microquasar companion stars. We constrain injection spectral indices using LHAASO proton, helium, and all-particle spectra, and use their data for model discrimination. Under the assumption of a unified heavy-element injection spectrum, Case-2 fits the data significantly better than Case-1, but at the cost of large, physically unmotivated differences between heavy-element and He injection indices. This suggests the need to either relax the unified heavy-element injection-index assumption or explore alternative abundance patterns. We predict that if the new-source composition follows Case-1, the individual elemental spectral indices before the knee should remain nearly consistent; if it follows Case-2, the Mg, Si, and Fe spectra will be significantly softer than those of He, C, and O. Future element-resolved energy-spectrum measurements by HERD and LHAASO will provide a decisive test of this discriminating feature.

Optical constraints on intranight variability in TeV HBLs from a two-year Asiago Schmidt monitoring campaign

Domenico Impiombato, Giacomo Bonnoli, Elisa Prandini, Luca Zampieri, Irene Albanese

We present a two-year homogeneous optical monitoring campaign of three TeV-emitting high-synchrotron-peaked BL Lac objects, 1ES 1959+650, H 1426+428, and PG 1553+113, carried out with the Asiago Schmidt 67/92 cm telescope. TeV blazars are key targets in high-energy astrophysics, relevant for relativistic jet physics, particle acceleration, multiwavelength variability studies, and, in selected cases, multimessenger programmes. In this context, dense optical monitoring provides a low-energy synchrotron tracer of jet activity over temporal baselines that are difficult to cover continuously with high-energy facilities. The dataset consists of more than 17,000 R- and I-band photometric measurements obtained over 957 source-filter observing sessions between 2024 February 1 and 2026 January 31, for a total monitoring time of about 420 h. We applied a uniform differential-photometry and classical F-test framework based on two independent comparison-star pairs. Robust IDV is detected only in H 1426+428, with 15 out of 43 analysed sessions classified as variable in the R band and 5 out of 40 in the I band. No significant IDV is detected in 1ES 1959+650 or PG 1553+113 within the adopted statistical framework. For H 1426+428, the corresponding duration-weighted duty cycles, computed using only variable sessions with a measured local characteristic timescale, are 28.0% in the R band and 7.0% in the I band. The inferred local extrapolated doubling/halving timescales are of the order of 5-15 min in the observer frame. These results indicate that rapid optical synchrotron variability in TeV HBLs is intermittent and state-dependent. They also show that robotic small- and medium-aperture optical facilities can provide valuable long-term support for high-energy monitoring programmes by supplying dense, uniform, and repeatable optical coverage.

A possible evolutionary connection between high-mass X-ray binary pulsars and isolated rotation-powered pulsars

Jun Yang

We investigate the long-term spin evolution of high-mass X-ray binary (HMXB) pulsars in the Small Magellanic Cloud (SMC) using archival measurements of spin periods and period derivatives from XMM-Newton, Chandra, and RXTE. In the P-|dP/dt| plane, the HMXB pulsars follow an approximately linear relation in log-log space with a slope of 1.02 +/- 0.11, similar to the trend shown by the youngest isolated rotation-powered pulsars (RPPs; e.g., Manchester et al., 2005; Lorimer and Kramer, 2005). For comparison, young RPPs from the ATNF Pulsar Catalogue give slopes that depend strongly on the characteristic-age cut: 1.10 +/- 0.16 for characteristic ages below 10,000 years and 0.72 +/- 0.08 for characteristic ages below 100,000 years. The full non-recycled population shows no significant correlation. The HMXB pulsars lie in the same characteristic-age range (approximately 1,000-10,000 years) as the youngest RPPs, but at spin periods two to three orders of magnitude longer. Reference characteristic-age contours in the HMXB P-|dP/dt| plane are roughly parallel to this relation, consistent with a continuous evolutionary sequence.

The compact jets of the black hole X-ray binary Swift J1753.5-0127 during its 2023-2024 outburst

Noa Grollimund, Stéphane Corbel, Lauren Rhodes, David M. Russell, M. Cristina Baglio, Francesco Carotenuto, Tristan Bouchet, Payaswini Saikia, Rob P. Fender, Sara E. Motta, Piergiorgio Casella, Kevin Alabarta, Fraser Lewis, Andrew K. Hughes, Fraser J. Cowie, Justine Crook-Mansour, Xian Zhang

X-ray binaries are excellent laboratories to study the coupling between accretion and ejection phenomena around compact objects through multiwavelength observations. Swift J1753.5-0127 is a black hole low-mass X-ray binary discovered in outburst in 2005 that remained active for 11 years, followed by two mini-outbursts. We investigate the behavior of the compact jets of Swift J1753.5-0127 during its 2023-2024 outburst. We report a dense multiwavelength monitoring in the radio, NIR/optical/UV and X-ray bands. Using new and archival luminosity measurements, we construct a refined, smoother radio/X-ray plane that covers the steep/outlier and shallow/transition branches of the radio/X-ray correlation. We also present spectral energy distributions (SEDs) constructed using quasi-simultaneous observations from four epochs. Swift J1753.5-0127 underwent a faint, hard-only, double-peaked outburst, followed by a re-brightening in the optical. The updated radio/X-ray plane offers a clear view on the region occupied by this source, similar to MAXI J1348-630, but at lower radio luminosities than H 1743-322, and we confirm its hybrid behavior. The broad-band SED fitting reveals that a cool, truncated accretion disk dominates the NIR/optical/UV band. We find that the compact jets have a variable but weak contribution in the NIR, with a break frequency $ν_\mathrm{break} \lesssim 5 \times 10^{12}~\mathrm{Hz}$, providing additional evidence that radio-quiet sources are also infrared-quiet. We also find evidence that the radio spectral index evolves with X-ray luminosity, transitioning from inverted values near quiescence to flatter or negative values at higher luminosities. In the context of adiabatic jet models, this trend may indicate that the jet geometry becomes increasingly confined as the accretion rate rises.

A0538-66: A transient ultraluminous X-ray source with a relativistic outflow, 69ms pulsations, and anomalous optical activity

Lorenzo Ducci, Sandro Mereghetti, Ciro Pinto, Matteo Bachetti, Motoki Nakajima, Rahul Sharma, Stefano Covino, Michela Rigoselli, Caterina Tresoldi, Tatehiro Mihara, Mutsumi Sugizaki

A0538-66 is a peculiar Be/X-ray binary in the Large Magellanic Cloud, hosting the fastest spinning neutron star of its class (~69ms) and showing the brightest optical bursts of any system of this type. We report four XMM-Newton observations obtained near periastron in 2025, together with MAXI and REM monitoring, and a reanalysis of archival RXTE data. On 30 September 2025 the source reached L_X~1.5E39 erg/s (0.2-10 keV), the highest level since 1980, and in February, May, and August 2026, MAXI detected similarly bright outbursts. During the XMM-Newton observation taken on 30 September 2025, we detected the pulsations, measuring P_spin=69.25878(2) ms and dP_spin/dt=6.58(9)E-10 s/s; the pulsed fraction was low (~3% in 1-10 keV), increased with energy, and the soft and hard pulse profiles were shifted by half a cycle. A new detection in RXTE data of 3 December 1998 gives a faster spin period of P_spin=68.88813(28) ms. The XMM-Newton/RGS spectra show emission lines from photoionised plasma at rest and blueshifted absorption lines that imply an outflow at ~0.12c. This indicates a highly super-Eddington accretion rate and an intrinsic L_X~1.5E39 erg/s, and it makes A0538-66 a transient ultra-luminous X-ray pulsar, a class of which only a few objects are known so far. Since 2023 the optical bursts observed by REM have become longer and brighter, resembling those of the 1980s, but most of them lack a luminous X-ray counterpart (L_X>1E37 erg/s). We interpret these results in terms of a radiation pressure dominated accretion disc whose geometrically thick inner regions intermittently screen the innermost emitting regions.

Observational evidence of black hole event horizon from ultra-compact X-ray binaries

Qian-Qi Ma, Wei-Min Gu, Shan-Shan Weng, Mouyuan Sun

The event horizon is the most crucial feature that distinguishes black hole (BH) from all other objects in the universe. However, since no radiation can escape from the event horizon, its direct observational evidence remains lacking. Although both are compact objects, the event horizon leads to differences in the radiative behavior between black hole and neutron star (NS) X-ray binaries. Historically efforts to probe the existence of event horizons revealed that the BH X-ray binaries are fainter than NSs with similar orbital periods in their quiescent states. Here, we use a special class of X-ray binaries namely the ultra-compact X-ray binaries (UCXBs), to reveal the existence of the event horizon near stellar-mass black holes. In short-period UCXBs, gravitational radiation dominates the orbital angular momentum loss, allowing us to accurately determine their accretion rates. Based on five UCXBs with orbital periods shorter than 40 min, located in globular clusters, and identified accretors (NS or BH), we obtained their accretion rates. Combining their accretion rates and X-ray luminosities, we calculated the X-ray radiative efficiencies of NS and BH. The radiative efficiency of BH is about two to three orders of magnitude lower than that of NS systems, indicating the accretion energy is swallowed by the BH, thereby indirectly probe the presence of the event horizon.

Scaling Up: A Global Optical/X-ray Correlation For Reprocessing Across Black Hole Masses

Changhyun Cho, David M. Russell, Aaryan Sharma, Payaswini Saikia, Maria Cristina Baglio, Kevin Alabarta, Sandeep K. Rout, Yi-Jung Yang, Eleonora Caruso, Mokhine Motsoaledi, Meriana Allah, Ian M. McHardy

This study extends the optical/X-ray correlation from black hole low-mass X-ray binaries (XRBs) to active galactic nuclei (AGN), bridging scales from stellar-mass to supermassive black holes (SMBHs). In XRBs, optical emission predominantly originates from the thermal reprocessing of high-energy photons on the surface of the geometrically thin, optically thick accretion disk. The reprocessed optical luminosity follows a power-law relation of the form $L_{V} \propto L_{X}^β a$, with $β\sim 0.5$, where $L_X$ is the X-ray luminosity and the orbital separation $a$ serves as a proxy for the disk size. To test the universality of this relationship across all mass scales, we analyzed quasi-simultaneous X-ray and optical flux data from a sample of XRBs and AGN that have estimates of the radius of the optically emitting disk. To bridge the structural differences between these populations, we utilized the characteristic optical/X-ray time lag derived from reverberation mapping as a physical proxy for the reprocessing radius. Our analysis reveals a tight, global correlation across all black hole masses, described by $\log L_V = 0.48\log L_X + 0.96\log a_R + 6.5$ with a scatter of $\sim 0.20$ dex, where $a_R$ is the reprocessing radius derived from the lag. Incorporating a geometric correction for the disk inclination angle ($\cos i$) slightly reduces this scatter to $0.18$ dex. These results suggest that X-ray reprocessing is scale-invariant across accretion disks, from stellar-mass black holes to SMBHs. This scaling relation provides new constraints for models of X-ray emission regions in accreting black holes.

Revisiting the Multiwavelength Data of GRB 240825A: Implications for a Two-Stage Central Engine and a Dusty Environment

Ren-Jie Xiong, Zi-Qi Wang, Xiao-Li Huang, En-Wei Liang

Reanalyzing the multi-wavelength data of GRB 240825A, we identify two distinct prompt-emission episodes, i.e. an early, spectrally soft plateau and a subsequent bright, spectrally hard gamma-ray pulse. We show that this signature is consistent with a two-stage central engine model, in which the early soft plateau is powered by the dipolar spin-down radiation of a transient proto-magnetar, while the later hard pulse arises from a relativistic jet launched by fallback accretion onto the nascent black hole via the Blandford-Znajek mechanism. Adopting simulation-motivated initial black hole (BH) masses ($M_{\rm BH,0}$) and spins ($a_0$), our fits to the second episode yield total masses supplied to the post-collapse disk of $M_{\rm fb}=0.45$--$0.96 M_\odot$, fallback radii of $r_{\rm fb}=16.9$--$17.6 r_g$, and characteristic fallback timescales of $t_{\rm fb}=0.13$--$0.25$ s. The corresponding maximum inner accretion rates range from $0.18$ to $0.70 M_\odot \mathrm{s}^{-1}$, which is sensitive to $a_0$. Joint spectral fits with optical-X-ray afterglow data reveal a coupled temporal evolution of dust extinction and equivalent hydrogen column density. Incorporating this extinction evolution, an external forward-reverse shock model in a wind-like medium can account for the radio-to-GeV band afterglow data. Although ambient dust and the bright host galaxy may obscure any associated supernova, late-time optical reddening remains compatible with a supernova contribution. Applying the empirical relation between the prompt spectral peak energy and the SN peak bolometric magnitude gives an illustrative estimate of $M_{\rm bol,peak}=-19.46\pm0.35$ mag for a putative associated SN. These results support a two-stage evolution of the central engine and variable dusty environment of GRB 240825A, which provides insights into the nature of its progenitor.

10 Oct 2026

Reawakening of GS 1843+00: A Multiwavelength Study of its 2026 Giant X-ray Outburst

Gaurava K. Jaisawal, Georgios Vasilopoulos, Francesco Carotenuto, Hannah C. I. Wichern

GS 1843+00 (Ginga 1843+009) is a poorly studied Be/X-ray binary system that underwent a major X-ray outburst in 2026 February-May after nearly sixteen years of quiescence. We present an X-ray, optical, and radio study of GS 1843+00 during this outburst, focusing on the evolution of the neutron star emission geometry, Be-disk connection, and possible outflow activity. We analyse three NuSTAR observations obtained at different stages of the outburst, complemented by archival RXTE and Chandra data, optical spectroscopy and imaging with the Nordic Optical Telescope, and a MeerKAT radio observation. NuSTAR observations reveal coherent X-ray pulsations around 29.7 s with a spin-up rate of $(-5.1\pm0.1)\times10^{-8}$ s s$^{-1}$ during the outburst. The pulse profiles show strong evolution with energy and outburst phase, with distinct morphologies even at comparable luminosities. The pulsed fraction varies between 1 and 16% and exhibits a complex reversal in energy dependence above 7 keV, increasing with energy during the first two observations and decreasing during the third. The 3-79 keV broadband spectra are described by thermal Comptonization with a blackbody and an Fe K$α$ emission line, with no evidence for a cyclotron absorption feature. Phase-resolved spectroscopy reveals variations in the Comptonization and blackbody parameters, indicating changes in the accretion-column structure. Optical spectroscopy confirms the presence of a Be-circumstellar disk through H$α$ and Paschen-series emission lines. No radio counterpart is detected, with a $3σ$ upper limit of 36 $μ$Jy beam$^{-1}$ at 1.28 GHz. Weaker H$α$ emission compared with previous epochs indicates that the Be-circumstellar disk was in a different state during the current episode, while the radio non-detection suggests that any associated outflow activity was faint or below the detection limit.

A Pipeline for the AI-Assisted Analysis of Imaging X-ray Polarimetry Explorer Observations of Black Hole X-ray Binaries and Limits on Their Polarization Variability

B. Domine, B. Liu, H. Krawczynski, M. Kundu, Shravan Vengalil Menon, Fang Zhou

The Imaging X-ray Polarimetry Explorer (IXPE) has opened a new window into the high-energy Universe, with the capability to measure the arrival time, arrival direction, energy, and, for the first time with the required sensitivity, the polarization of X-rays from black holes, neutron stars, and other cosmic sources of X-rays. In this paper, we discuss the use of Artificial Intelligence (AI) agents for the analysis of IXPE data. The pipeline combines an immutable code base with evolving knowledge databases. The generation of analysis projects makes use of a literature data base to judge the novelty of the proposed analysis projects. We emphasize that the analysis requires two types of expert knowledge: detailed instructions about how to reduce the X-ray data, and expert knowledge about how to interpret the results. We employ several levels of auditors that scrutinize the analysis plan, the analysis scripts, and the analysis results for data and method adequacy. The pipeline includes a staggered approach to interpret the analysis results. We present exemplary results from the pipeline applied to IXPE observations of Black Hole X-ray Binaries (BHXRBs) including comparisons of results from the analysis pipeline with published results and a systematic search for polarization degree and polarization angle variability in long IXPE observations. We critically discuss the performance of the pipeline and future avenues for improving it.

A first view of interstellar Fe-bearing dust grains with XRISM

I. Psaradaki, L. Corrales, E. Costantini, M. Mehdipour, D. Rogantini, H. Takahasi, R. Ballhausen, P. Chakraborty, T. Nakagawa, R. K. Smith, S. Zeegers

Understanding the reservoirs of iron (Fe) in our Galaxy is crucial for probing the mechanisms that drive dust grain growth in the interstellar medium (ISM). High-resolution X-ray absorption spectroscopy offers a powerful tool to investigate the chemistry of dust grains, particularly those containing Fe, through spectroscopic features near photoabsorption edges known as X-ray Absorption Fine Structures (XAFS). With the Resolve spectrometer aboard XRISM, we can now examine the Fe K-edge (7.1 keV) owing to its improved spectral resolution in this energy range. This enabled us to probe the dust grain chemistry in high Galactic column densities. In this work, we present a pilot study on dust grain chemistry through the Fe K-edge of the XRISM Performance Verification target GX 340+0. This source is a persistently bright low-mass X-ray binary with a hydrogen column density of 6.6x10^22 cm^-2 along the line of sight, providing sufficient optical depth at the Fe K-edge. By fitting combined dust and gas models, we find that Fe is depleted by 95% into dust along the line of sight towards the source. Moreover, we find evidence that 38% of the solid-phase Fe is in metallic iron, 47% in iron sulfides, and the remainder in silicates. The large contribution from metallic iron and iron sulfides differs from what is typically inferred from X-ray studies of diffuse ISM and may suggest that that these compounds become more significant reservoirs of Fe in higher column density Galactic sightlines.

Accreting Neutron Stars in Low-Mass X-ray Binaries

M. C. Baglio, S. E. Motta, A. P. Nitindala, J. Poutanen

Neutron star low-mass X-ray binaries (NS LMXBs) are systems in which a neutron star accretes matter from a Roche-lobe-filling companion via an accretion disc, releasing gravitational energy with an efficiency of order 10-20% of the rest-mass energy. Unlike black holes, neutron stars possess a solid surface and a magnetic field, making NS LMXBs uniquely rich laboratories for high-energy astrophysics and dense-matter physics. In this chapter we review the current understanding of accretion onto neutron stars in LMXBs, tracing matter from the outer disc radii to the stellar surface across five decades of observational discovery. We survey the principal classes of accreting neutron star systems - canonical atoll and Z-sources, ultracompact X-ray binaries, accreting millisecond pulsars, and transitional millisecond pulsars - and discuss how their spectral states, timing properties, and multiwavelength behaviour encode the physics of the inner accretion flow. We examine thermonuclear Type-I X-ray bursts as probes of nuclear burning and neutron star structure, and review the boundary and spreading layers where accreted matter decelerates onto the stellar surface. We discuss relativistic jets and ionised disc winds as regulators of the accretion-ejection cycle. Finally, we present X-ray polarimetric results from the Imaging X-ray Polarimetry Explorer (IXPE), which for the first time provide new, though model-dependent, constraints on the geometry of the emission regions - disc, corona, spreading layer, and jets - that spectroscopy alone cannot resolve, alongside complementary optical and infrared polarimetric observations.

Changing-look Active Galactic Nuclei from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument. VII. Testing Disk-Corona Diagnostics with eROSITA

Wei-Jian Guo, Shuo Zhai, Zhu-Heng Yao, Huaqing Cheng, Jingwei Hu, Jun-Jie Jin

Changing-look active galactic nuclei (CL-AGN) have been proposed to follow an X-ray-binary-like, V-shaped relation between the optical/ultraviolet-to-X-ray spectral slope, $α_{\rm OX}$, and the Eddington ratio, $λ_{\rm Edd}\equiv L_{\rm bol}/L_{\rm Edd}$. We test this interpretation using CL-AGN selected from the Dark Energy Spectroscopic Instrument (DESI) and control samples matched to the eROSITA all-sky survey (eRASS). We find that the apparent positive correlation between $α_{\rm OX}$ and the optical-continuum-based Eddington ratio, $λ_{\rm Edd,opt}$, is not unique to CL-AGN, but also appears in matched non-changing-look samples. In broad-H$α$ AGN, our main statistical sample, this structure is largely driven by the known dependence of $α_{\rm OX}$ on ultraviolet luminosity and by the covariance between the rest-frame 2500 Angstrom luminosity, $L_{2500}$, the black-hole mass, $M_{\rm BH}$, and $λ_{\rm Edd,opt}$. When an X-ray-based Eddington ratio is used instead, the relation reverses sign because the X-ray luminosity, $L_X$, enters the two axes with opposite signs. An apparent $α_{\rm OX}$-Eddington-ratio V-shape can therefore be induced by the luminosity estimator and is not a standalone accretion-state diagnostic. As a complementary test, we examine the eRASS effective photon index, $Γ_{\rm eff}$, inferred from catalogue band fluxes. In broad-line AGN, it shows a weak positive association with the X-ray-independent optical Eddington ratio. However, the weaker hard-band trend depends on the null model and does not isolate coronal softening. These results motivate further X-ray spectroscopy, combined with independently estimated Eddington ratios, to test for intrinsic changes in the disk-corona system.

06 Oct 2026

The Potential Contribution of Radiative Supernova Remnants to the Local Cosmic Ray Spectrum

P. Cristofari, V. H. M. Phan, S. Recchia, S. Gabici

Recent observations of very-high-energy gamma rays from massive stellar clusters and microquasars have demonstrated that these systems can efficiently accelerate particles to very high energies, renewing the debate on the dominant sources of Galactic cosmic rays (CRs) and casting further doubt on the role of supernova remnants (SNRs), long considered the prime candidates. In these astrophysical environments, particle acceleration is generally thought to be driven by diffusive shock acceleration (DSA). The question of the duration of the phase in which DSA can efficiently take place is therefore essential to clarify the contribution of the various candidate CR sources. We aim at clarifying the contribution of SNRs to the Galactic CR spectrum, and the expected efficiency of DSA, especially accounting for possible particle acceleration during part of the radiative phase. We compute the total CR proton content at typical SNRs from DSA and reaccelerated pre-existing CRs, and discuss the importance of these two mechanisms throughout the adiabatic and early radiative phases. We find that if DSA and reacceleration take place during a fraction of the radiative phase, the total content of particles energized at SNRs can quickly overcome the typical budget required to account for the bulk of CRs. This suggests that the typical instantaneous efficiency, often defined as the fraction of fluid ram pressure converted into CRs would need to remain below $\lesssim 1$\% for most of the SNR evolution. Moreover, the radiative phase can contribute to shaping the spectra injected from typical SNRs. We illustrate that the difference between SNRs from thermonuclear and core-collapse SNe could help account for the measured difference in the helium and proton of the local interstellar spectrum.

Uncovering the Physical Origins of Black Holes in the Milky Way for Astronomical Surveys

Ilia Kiato, Pierson Lipschultz, Vicky Kalogera, Jeff J. Andrews, Max M. Briel, Abhishek Chattaraj, Seth Gossage, Philipp M. Srivastava

The observational landscape of stellar-mass black holes (BHs) in the Milky Way is expanding beyond X-ray binaries, with Gaia DR4 and Roman expected to open new windows onto BH populations. We use the binary population synthesis framework POSYDON to predict the present-day Galactic BH population and connect it to its evolutionary origins. We test the robustness of these predictions to uncertainties in binary evolution and BH formation, including core-collapse mechanisms, natal kicks, common-envelope efficiency, metallicity, and initial binary distributions. Across our models, we find that most Galactic BHs are isolated, with stellar mergers providing the largest contribution and binary disruptions the second largest. Among surviving binaries, detached BH-BH systems dominate. We identify a distinct, narrow feature at higher BH masses ($\simeq13\,\mathrm{M}_{\odot}$ in the fiducial model) produced by wind-driven evolution of massive Wolf-Rayet progenitors. This feature is particularly prominent among detached BH-BH binaries and persists across variations in binary-evolution and BH-formation physics, but depends strongly on metallicity. At lower masses, these BH-BH systems predominantly originate from progenitors that undergo stable mass transfer before BH formation. Finally, for more direct observational applications, we embed the synthetic populations in a Galactic model, evolve their kinematics, perform first-order microlensing calculations for BH-BH lenses, and examine BH systems with luminous companions in the context of the Gaia BHs. In the latter, we uncover a robust stable-mass-transfer population largely absent in rapid population-synthesis predictions. These results provide an evolutionary framework for interpreting the emerging Galactic BH population across complementary observational channels.

Energy-independent X-Ray Polarization in 4U 1630-47 in the Intermediate State from Spectroscopy and Polarimetry

Elena Seifina, Lev Titarchuk, Paolo Soffitta, Lorenzo Marra, Fiamma Capitanio, Fabio Muleri, Anastasiia Emelianova

The IXPE mission enabled measurements of X-ray polarization in accreting binaries. The dependence of the X-ray polarization degree on the binary inclination and the optical depth of the emitting medium $τ_0$ are the new important tools in X-ray Astronomy. We analyzed the black hole X-ray binary 4U~1630--47 as an example of one of the enigmatic sources in our Galaxy. We consider spectral and polarimetric observations of outburst episodes from 4U~1630--47 observed with the IXPE and the NICER. We carefully investigated a time interval of the IXPE observations during which 4U~1630--47 was in a single spectral state (the intermediate state) and found that the polarization degree did not change significantly during this time interval with the photon energy in the 2--8 keV range ($P\sim$ 6\%). We interpreted this energy independence behavior in $P(E)$ as a result of the Comptonization (up-scattering) of soft photons in the flat Compton cloud. We argued that the X-ray source broadband energy spectra can be reproduced by a physical model composed of the Comptonized component and iron-line ({\it Gaussian}) component. Spectral analysis using this model shows that during the intermediate state the 4U~1630--47 spectrum was characterized by a photon index $Γ\sim 2.5$ and the electron temperature $kT_e\sim ~9 ~{\rm keV}$. In fact, this approach allows one to easily find the half-optical depth $τ_0\sim 1.8$ of the Compton cloud in 4U~1630--47 applying the inclination $69\pm1^\circ$ and using the relation $(P,\cos(i),τ_0)$ when solving the full radiative transfer equation for a flat Compton cloud by an iterative method. This approach is also accompanied by the fact that the polarization degree is independent of the photon energy, which we demonstrate observationally, at least for 4U~1630--47 in the 2--8 keV range.

Pulsations and constraints on time lags in two distinct ULXs: NGC 5204 X-1 and NGC 55 ULX-1

Rajath Sathyaprakash, Matthew J. Middleton, Chris Done

The nature of ultra-luminous X-ray sources (ULXs) is shrouded in mystery as they're believed to be point sources with luminosities above the classical Eddington limit for a 10$M_{\odot}$ black hole (BH), but generally fit into the classification tree of super-Eddington X-ray binaries. Studies of reverberation lags and lag-energy spectra provide some insight into the nature of viscous turbulence in optically thick, super-Eddington discs, and are useful in conjunction with modelling multi-wavelength spectra and covariance spectra. In this study, we focus on the timing properties of two classically distinct ULXs: NGC 5204 X-1 and NGC 55 ULX-1, both of which are known to emit relativistic winds, as observed by modelling emission line spectra with photo-ionisation models such as SPEX, but are thought to be viewed at slightly different inclinations due to differences in the hardness-ratio of their X-ray continuum. Here, we perform an extensive timing study of the two ULXs to reveal new constraints on the accretion disc size scale and structure. We also perform an accelerated search for pulsations (including the correction for an orbital modulation of the signal) to discover significant spin-periodicities in both NGC 5204 X-1 and NGC 55 ULX-1 (at $> 3σ$ significance).

02 Oct 2026

A concordance model of neutron star kicks

Cheyanne Shariat, Kareem El-Badry, Smadar Naoz

Neutron stars (NSs) receive natal kicks during supernovae that affect their survival in binaries and motion through the Galaxy. The global distribution of these kick velocities across the NS population remains uncertain. Here, we combine the orbital properties and birth rates of Gaia NS and Be+NS binaries with velocity constraints from young isolated pulsars to construct a concordance model of NS natal kicks. We find that all three populations can be jointly described by a shared bimodal kick distribution. The low-velocity component follows a lognormal distribution with $μ_{\ln v}=1.87^{+0.65}_{-0.53}$ and $σ_{\ln v}=0.55^{+0.85}_{-0.44}$, corresponding to a median speed of $6.5^{+5.9}_{-2.7}~{\rm km~s^{-1}}$. The high-velocity component is consistent with the distribution inferred from young pulsars, which peaks near $200~{\rm km~s^{-1}}$. The low-velocity component contributes only $\sim10\%$ of all NS births, but produces most Gaia NS binaries and roughly half of Be+NS binaries. The same model predicts systemic recoil velocities consistent with the observed kinematics of Gaia NSs, Be+NS binaries, and NS low-mass X-ray binaries. For Gaia NSs, whose formation history remains unknown, we use this kick distribution to constrain their progenitors. We favor pre-supernova binaries containing intermediate-mass stripped stars with $\sim1~{\rm M_\odot}$ companions in au-scale orbits, and predict $\sim300$ such binaries in the Milky Way today, $\sim40$ in the LMC, and $\sim30$ in the SMC. Finally, we consider the implications of these kicks for NSs in hierarchical triples. We find that 2-10$\%$ of Gaia NS triples and 3-23$\%$ of Be+NS triples retain their tertiary after the supernova. Overall, our results suggest that a shared natal kick law can account for isolated pulsars and surviving NS binaries, where most NSs receive large kicks and low kicks are common among surviving binaries.

Classification of X-ray Binaries in NGC 6946 with the use of Multiwavelength Data

Senay Avdan, Hasan Avdan, Eda Sonbas, Kalvir S. Dhuga

We identified X-ray binary (XRB) candidates in the galaxy NGC 6946 using archival {\it Chandra} data. A total of 98 XRB candidates were detected within the 0.3$-$8 keV energy range, with luminosities between 10$^{36}$ and 10$^{40}$ erg~s$^{-1}$. X-ray color-color diagrams were constructed for these sources, classifying 57 as high-mass X-ray binaries (HMXBs) and 8 as low-mass X-ray binaries (LMXBs). The X-ray temporal variability of the sources was analyzed, revealing that 40 sources exhibit variability and 26 are classified as transient sources. Possible optical counterparts for 57 sources were identified using {\it Hubble Space Telescope} ({\it HST}) images. Based on the color-magnitude diagrams (CMDs), 40 sources were found to have masses greater than 8M$_{\odot}$ and were classified as HMXBs. Seventeen sources with masses between 5 and 8M$_{\odot}$ were classified as intermediate-mass X-ray binaries (IMXBs). The remaining sources, for which optical counterparts could not be identified, were assumed to be LMXBs. The X-ray luminosity functions (XLFs) derived for HMXBs and LMXBs, identified through both X-ray and optical classification, were best described by a single power-law model ($α\sim$ 1.3$-$1.6). We supplemented traditional classification methods with a machine learning (ML) approach based on multiwavelength photometry. While traditional methods identified no AGN candidates, the ML approach detected a significant AGN population. The spatial resolution of the observing instruments and the limits of the training database hinder the reliability of the ML classifications, thus highlighting a key challenge in combining these methods for extragalactic X-ray source

XMM-Newton Resolves Parsec-scale X-ray Jets in the PeVatron Microquasar V4641 Sgr

Naomi Tsuji, Caterina Tresoldi, Kaya Mori, Hiromasa Suzuki, Matthew Lundy, Dmitry Khangulyan, Samar Safi-Harb, Laura Olivera-Nieto, Felix Aharonian, Shotaro Abe, Zhen Cao, Sabrina Casanova, Maria Chernyakova, Yi-Heng Chi, Stephane Corbel, E. de la Fuente, Hua Feng, Tatsuki Fujiwara, Noa Grollimund, Dezhi Huang, Yoshiyuki Inoue, Ryohei Kanazashi, Shigeo S. Kimura, Marianne Lemoine-Goumard, Jonathan Mackey, Alessio Marino, Samaresh Mondal, Kai Morita, Maxime Parra, Gabriele Ponti, Brian Reville, Megumi Shidatsu, Shunya Takekawa, Michelle Tsirou, Xiaojie Wang, Heshou Zhang, Ping Zhou

The microquasar V4641 Sagittarii (V4641 Sgr) has recently been established as one of the most powerful Galactic PeV cosmic-ray accelerators (PeVatrons) through the detection of degree-scale gamma-ray emission to 0.8 PeV. Using XMM-Newton, we performed an X-ray survey of its gamma-ray-emitting regions during the quiescent phase. We discovered extended X-ray emission to the north and south of V4641 Sgr, aligned with the radio-jet axis, revealing X-ray jet structures extending over several tens of parsecs for the first time. The northern and southern knot-like features, dubbed n1 and s1, are located approximately 20 pc from the central binary and spatially coincident with the gamma-ray emission. The X-ray emission from each knot, characterized by a photon index of $Γ_X = 1.9 \pm 0.1_{\rm stat} \pm 0.5_{\rm syst}$ and an energy flux of $\sim$(2--4)$\times10^{-13}$~erg~cm$^{-2}$~s$^{-1}$ in the 2--10 keV band, is likely non-thermal in origin, indicating synchrotron radiation from accelerated electrons. The morphological coincidence between the X-ray and gamma-ray emission favors a leptonic scenario for the gamma rays, although the X-ray emission is more compact than the gamma-ray-emitting region and the hadronic scenario cannot be excluded. Through X-ray to gamma-ray modeling, we obtain a weak magnetic field strength of 3--5~$μ$G. We propose that the newly detected X-ray knots represent particle-acceleration sites, such as jet termination or (re-)collimation regions powered by V4641 Sgr, or downstream structures analogous to the knots of the outer jets in SS~433/W50. This discovery suggests that extended X-ray emission along jets would be a key signature, yet previously overlooked, in PeVatron microquasars.

Candidate X-ray Counterparts of Ultra-High-Energy Emission of the Galactic Microquasar V4641 Sgr Revealed by Einstein Probe

Yi-Heng Chi, Shao-Qiang Xi, Ping Zhou, Hua Feng, Ruo-Yu Liu, Songzhan Chen, Naomi Tsuji, Caterina Tresoldi, Gabriele Ponti, Zhen Cao, Yong Chen, Shumei Jia, Chi-Chuan Jin, Chengkui Li, Yuan Liu, Matthew Lundy, Kaya Mori, Haiwu Pan, Lian Tao, Junfeng Wang, Weimin Yuan, Juan Zhang, Haisheng Zhao

Microquasars have emerged as promising PeVatron candidates capable of accelerating particles to PeV energies, with recent observations revealing ultra-high-energy (UHE) $γ$-ray emission from extended structures associated with these systems. Multi-wavelength studies of such extended structures are crucial for understanding the acceleration, transport, and radiation processes of UHE cosmic rays (CRs), yet observational evidence remains limited. Here, we report the discovery of diffuse X-ray structures spatially coincident with the TeV $γ$-ray emission regions to the north and south of the microquasar V4641 Sgr, using the Follow-up X-ray Telescope (FXT) onboard the Einstein Probe. We identify X-ray structures located $\sim20$-$40$ pc from V4641 Sgr, extending over several arcminutes. These structures exhibit X-ray fluxes of $\sim1$-$3\times10^{-13}$ erg s$^{-1}$ cm$^{-2}$ and photon indices of $Γ\sim2$-3 in 0.5-8 keV per individual knot. Under the one-zone leptonic interpretation, the weak X-ray emission implies a magnetic field of order $1-2 μ$G. Stronger fields remain possible if only part of the TeV-PeV emission is produced by inverse Compton scattering, with the remaining emission arising from hadronic interactions. The EP-FXT observations support the presence of relativistic electrons on tens-of-parsec scales, while the origin of the UHE $γ$-ray emission remains open.

The Impact of a Clumpy Ambient Medium on the Dynamics and Synchrotron Emission of AGN Jets

Ivan Almeida, Christian Fendt, Bhargav Vaidya

Relativistic jets from AGN are expected to propagate through an inhomogeneous ISM, but the role of small-scale gas inhomogeneities in shaping their early evolution remains uncertain. We investigate this problem using 3D special-relativistic magnetohydrodynamic simulations with PLUTO, following a jet with a Lorentz factor of 10 propagating through a pc-scale medium. We compare a homogeneous ambient medium with two clumpy ambient-medium models, motivated by dense gas in the nuclear ISM, in which clouds occupy volume filling factors of $0.1$ and $1$ per cent. We compute synthetic synchrotron maps and spectra from Lagrangian macro-particles that have been accelerated by diffusive shock acceleration. Jet-cloud interactions deflect the flow, enhance shock formation, and produce a more asymmetric cocoon than in the homogeneous ISM case. Altogether, this results in a substantially stronger mass loading: the entrained mass increases from $0.43$ M$_{\odot}$ in the homogeneous run to $5.3$ and $8.8$ M$_{\odot}$ in the clumpy runs. The clumpy ISM also modifies the turbulence and the material mixing within the jet cocoon. The dynamical differences we find translate into brighter and more irregular synchrotron emission. For the same injected jet, the models with a clumpy medium produce stronger frequency-integrated synchrotron emission than the homogeneous run, with the largest differences in the SED occurring from the sub-mm to the infrared/optical bands. Our results demonstrate that including a small-scale ISM structure provides a more complete description of the early dynamical evolution of young AGN jets and their radiative properties.

25 Years of Mrk 421 with XMM-Newton: Unveiling Structured Jets and Energy-Dependent Escape

Tek P. Adhikari, Gopal Bhatta, Sangeetha Kizhakkekalam, Navaneeth P K, Alex Markowitz, Zhicheng He, Santanu Mondal

We present a long-term X-ray spectral and timing analysis of the TeV blazar Mrk~421 based on nearly a quarter century of archival \textit{XMM-Newton}/pn observations obtained between 2000 and 2025. Our sample comprises 76 epochs obtained with the pn instrument in both IMAGING and TIMING modes, allowing us to trace the source's variability and spectral evolution. Mrk~421 exhibits flux variations by approximately an order of magnitude, with prominent flaring periods interspersed with low-activity states. A clear harder-when-brighter trend is observed, accompanied by scatter in the hardness ratio (HR), suggesting flux alone does not uniquely determine the spectral state. Fractional variability in the hard band ($2-10$ keV) correlates tightly with that in the soft band ($0.3-2$ keV), with a steeper slope indicating pronounced variability at higher energies. The flux distribution is non-Gaussian and lognormal-like, dominated by moderate flux levels with a tail of bright flares. Spectral analysis shows that power-law model is not sufficient; 59 epochs are best described by a broken power-law (BPL) and 17 by a log-parabola (LP). This fluctuation between BPL and LP models suggests the emission region transitions between a distinct acceleration shockfront and downstream stochastic turbulence. The spectral break energy of BPL remains predominantly clustered around $2$ keV across all flux states, while the average spectral steepening above the break is mild ($ΔΓ\approx 0.19$), inconsistent with standard one-zone cooling models. Our results support structured jet scenarios or energy-dependent electron escape, providing key constraints on the long-term behavior of relativistic jets in high-synchrotron-peaked blazars.

Superorbital Phase Evolution and Identification of New Anomalous Low State Candidates for Her X-1

Yi Chou, Jun-Lei Wu

The 35-day superorbital modulation of the X-ray binary Her X-1 was systematically investigated using a 35-year multi-instrument X-ray observational baseline. We developed a Rank-Searching method using the peak intensity of the main high state to identify anomalous low states (ALSs) with an objective and reproducible criterion. The method recovers the known ALSs, identifies two previously unrecognized candidate episodes, ALS 1991 and ALS 2015, and independently recovers the recently reported ALS 2025. We then characterized the long-term superorbital phase evolution, identifying seven epochs that alternate between smooth and variable behavior. The smooth component is empirically described by a cubic trend, while the recurrent variable epochs have a characteristic timescale of approximately 3400 days. Within a first-order tilted-disk framework, the secular phase evolution corresponds to an inferred cumulative disk-mass increase of $\sim1.8\%$ and an outer-disk-radius expansion of $\sim0.9\%$, while the short-term phase excursions can be reproduced by disk-mass variations of $\lesssim2.4\%$. A viscous diffusion timescale of approximately 168 days provides a characteristic timescale for the short-term disk response. Finally, the stable phase relation and high profile similarity between the main and short high states indicate that the warped disk maintains a remarkably stable global geometry over the observational baseline.
