Only fast variability is not observed possibly smoothed by a dense circumstellar envelope. A physical scenario is consistently modeled that also gives credibility to its likely connection with LHAASO J1831-1007u*, an ultra-high-energy source in its immediate vicinity. Conclusions. Our reported identification not only helps to fill a gap in Galactic taxonomy, but also potentially strengthens the role of the microblazar and microquasar families in our understanding of the most energetic Milky Way phenomena.

X-ray Flares in Gamma-Ray Bursts at High and Very-High-Energies

Pawan Tiwari, Biswajit Banerjee, Gor Oganesyan, Ansh Chopra, Annarita Ierardi, Marica Branchesi, Stefano Ascenzi, Maria Edvige Ravasio

A significant fraction of GRB early afterglows exhibit fast and bright X-ray flares, discovered by Swift X-ray Telescope. Their rapid temporal variability and spectral evolution have suggested an internal shock origin, analogous to prompt MeV emission. However, their physical origin and radiation mechanism remain debated. High-energy (> 100 MeV) and very-high-energy (> 30 GeV) gamma-ray observations provide a powerful probe of flare dissipation region, constraining its size, magnetic field strength, and particle acceleration under the synchrotron self-Compton scenario in optically thin relativistic jets. We present a systematic multi-wavelength study of 66 X-ray flares from 47 GRBs observed by X-ray Telescope over 17 years, all within field of view of Fermi Large Area Telescope. We investigate their GeV counterparts and find that only five flares exhibit significant high-energy emission (> 3 sigma). Broadband spectral modeling indicates that this GeV emission is consistent with standard forward shock afterglow. We further investigate correlations between flare spectral properties and energy fluxes at 1 keV, 10 keV, and 1 GeV. Using a synchrotron self-Compton model, we constrain the physical conditions of the emitting region, including magnetic field strength, bulk Lorentz factor, and emission radius. For the most stringent GeV upper limits, we find a magnetic-to-electron luminosity ratio greater than or equal to 1, implying a highly magnetized emitting region. We predict very-high-energy gamma-ray emission from X-ray flares at early (~500s) and late (~5000s) times and assess their detectability with Cherenkov Telescopes. We find that later X-ray flares provide the most promising targets for follow-up observations owing to improved observational accessibility, sensitivity, and reduced response-time constraints of facilities such as Cherenkov Telescope Array Observatory.

31 Aug 2026

Evidence for a luminosity-class dichotomy in the supergiant donors of persistent high-mass X-ray binaries and supergiant fast X-ray transients

Lara Sidoli, Vito Sguera

We have investigated the properties of the supergiant donors in two types of high-mass X-ray binaries (HMXBs, i.e. the so-called supergiant fast X-ray transients (SFXTs) and the classical X-ray sources), by collecting all available information from the literature. A prevalence of O-type versus B-type supergiant donors in SFXTs was previously suggested in the literature, possibly indicative of faster winds in SFXTs than in classical, persistent sources. Our aim is to investigate whether there is evidence for a dichotomy in the stellar luminosity class, since B-type supergiants are also known to be hosted in SFXTs. Our collection of the relevant literature allowed us to build a robust sample of both types of HMXBs with a secure stellar classification. We have found that the SFXTs showing the most extreme, transient X-ray emission host only O-type or B-type stars with a Ib and Iab luminosity class, whereas classical sources have mostly Ia donors. This is robust evidence that might explain the two different behaviours of HMXBs at X-rays since the stellar luminosity class appears to be linked to the wind properties (the supergiant wind terminal velocity, the mass-loss rate, and the wind strength Q parameter), indicative of faster and less dense winds in SFXTs than in classical sources.

27 Oct 2026

Weighing dark matter particles with gravitational waves

Rodrigo Vicente, Francisco Duque, Theophanes K. Karydas, Gianfranco Bertone

It is commonly assumed that the mass of dark matter particles cannot be determined from their gravitational effects when dark matter behaves as collisionless matter. Here we show that extreme-mass-ratio inspirals observable by millihertz gravitational-wave observatories such as the Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA) could measure the mass of ultralight dark matter particles in sufficiently dense dark matter spikes. The separation of scales between the two black holes allows ultralight dark matter to form a collisionless density spike around the massive primary while retaining wave effects in its interaction with the stellar-mass companion. We incorporate wave-dependent accretion and dynamical friction into relativistic inspiral waveforms and jointly infer the dark matter particle mass, spike normalisation and binary parameters from simulated LISA signals. For massive black holes at the low-mass end of the LISA population ($M\sim 10^5 M_\odot$), our forecasts show that particle masses spanning $10^{-14}-10^{-11}\,\mathrm{eV}$ could be measured to within an order of magnitude.

A Quiet Night in the Millihertz Sky? LISA Forecasts from LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA Populations

Ignacio Magaña Hernandez, Antonella Palmese

The high-mass black hole population inferred from ground-based observations can substantially affect the number of binaries detectable by the Laser Interferometer Space Antenna (LISA). We use population constraints based on data from the latest LIGO/Virgo/KAGRA Gravitational-Wave Transient Catalog (GWTC-5.0), as well as constraints from the O3 search for intermediate-mass black hole binaries, to forecast the number and masses of binary black holes detectable by LISA. Using the released GWTC-5.0 population posterior, we obtain $6.6^{+5.5}_{-2.2}$ expected detections with signal-to-noise ratio $>8$ in 4 years (median and $90\%$ credible interval). The alternative population models we consider use a broken power law with two or three Gaussian peaks for either the primary-mass distribution or a common mass distribution for both black holes. For the three-peak model with the common mass distribution, extrapolating from $300$ to $1000M_\odot$ roughly doubles the median expected number of LISA detections to $\sim14$. The upper end of its $90\%$ credible interval rises from tens to thousands of expected detections, although approximations may limit the reliability of the most extreme counts. For the reference GWTC-5.0 population, $39^{+22}_{-11}\%$ of the binaries detectable by LISA merge during the mission or within $10$ years after its end and are potential multiband sources. Essentially all of these mergers would be detectable by a reference Cosmic Explorer configuration if it is operating at merger. Ground-based measurements of the high-mass population can therefore inform both LISA forecasts and the prospects for observing the same binaries through inspiral and merger.

Effects of Dust in Electron-Capture Supernovae: Difficulty in Explaining Intermediate Luminosity Red Transients

Yuma Takeichi, Daichi Tsuna, Daichi Hiramatsu, Morgan MacLeod, Masato Sato

The progenitors of intermediate-luminosity red transients (ILRTs) spanning the luminosity gap between novae and supernovae are under debate, and electron-capture supernovae (ECSNe) have been proposed as a strong candidate. ECSNe from super asymptotic giant branch (super-AGB) stars have been theoretically predicted to be much brighter than ILRTs, but attenuation by dusty winds of their super-AGB progenitors may ease this tension. Here we construct a semi-analytical framework for calculating the light curves of ECSNe with dust attenuation and re-emission, by combining recent ECSN light curve predictions with a model for dust physics. Even by varying both the wind mass-loss rate and the ECSN model parameters, we find it challenging to simultaneously fit the light curve and color evolution of ILRTs. This poses a potential challenge to the standard models of ECSNe for explaining ILRTs. For explaining ILRTs, we propose either the existence of a population of ECSNe with much lower explosion energies ($\sim$10$^{48}$-$10^{49}$ erg) and dimmer plateaus, or alternative model(s) to ECSNe that realize such low explosion energies.

Faint and Fast Transients from Double Detonations of Low Mass White Dwarfs

Miranda Pikus, Abigail Polin, Dillon Hasenour, Paul Duffell

There is significant evidence that white dwarfs can explode via the double detonation mechanism, whereby an initial detonation in an accreted helium shell triggers a second detonation of the carbon-oxygen core. This scenario has been invoked for white dwarfs near a solar mass to produce Type Ia-like supernovae, but low-mass explosions have been relatively unexplored despite the most common white dwarf being near 0.6 solar masses. We predict faint and fast transients emerging from double detonations of white dwarfs with total masses around 0.6 solar masses and accreted helium shells up to 0.3 solar masses. The models are computed through the moving-mesh nuclear hydrodynamics code Cattails and the Monte Carlo radiation transport code Sedona. A continuum of core-powered and shell-powered transients are produced with no more than 0.03 solar masses of radioactive nickel in the core or shell material, respectively. The light curves are rapid and sub-luminous, peaking near -17 magnitudes within 5 to 10 days. The ejecta is dominated by intermediate mass elements and unburnt core that imprint noticeable features in the spectrum. This is the first parameter survey to investigate double detonations at such low masses, and these systems could produce a historically missed population of supernovae. Our predictive models could help large sky surveys find these events.

Effects of Short-Range Correlations on Quarkyonic Matter

K. Folias, Ch. C. Moustakidis

In this work, we focus on investigating the impact of the short-range correlations on the properties of quarkyonic matter. These correlations induce the scattering of nucleons to momenta exceeding the Fermi momentum, thereby altering the momentum-space occupation distribution that underlies the quarkyonic framework. In particular, we examine to what extent the emergence of a high-momentum tail, due to the short-range nucleon-nucleon correlations, in the momentum distribution, for momenta exceeding the Fermi momentum, affects the fundamental properties of quarkyonic matter, such as the energy density, pressure, and speed of sound, among others. In fact, at high densities, the behavior of the momentum distribution has been established, both experimentally and theoretically, to follow a characteristic $\sim 1/k^4$ dependence at large momenta. To the best of our knowledge, this effect on quarkyonic matter has not yet been systematically investigated. Moreover, this experimentally confirmed feature of the momentum distribution suggests that heavy-ion collisions may probe quarkyonic matter, underscoring the importance of incorporating short-range correlations into a complete quarkyonic model.

Quasinormal Ringing of a Schwarzschild Black Hole Surrounded by a King Dark Matter Halo: Higher-Order WKB Analysis

Semra Gurtas Dogan, Hassan Hassanabadi, Abdullah Guvendi

We study scalar and electromagnetic perturbations of a Schwarzschild black hole (BH) surrounded by a dark matter halo described by the King density profile. The lapse function can be expressed as $1-2[M+M_{\rm DM}(r)]/r$, where $M_{\rm DM}(r)$ is the halo mass enclosed within radius $r$; thus, the halo affects the geometry through its enclosed-mass function. We compute the quasinormal frequencies using the sixth-order WKB method and its Padé-improved version. Rewriting the lapse function in terms of the inverse hyperbolic sine avoids the severe numerical cancellations that arise when evaluating its high-order derivatives. For the configurations examined, the fundamental frequencies agree with those extracted from time-domain evolution to within $0.2\%$.The halo lowers both the oscillation frequency and the damping rate. The change of the real part is almost entirely accounted for by the halo mass enclosed by the photon sphere, and the additional decrease of the damping rate follows the ratio of the Lyapunov exponent to the angular frequency of the circular null orbit. Since part of the halo lies inside the horizon in this metric, we also place the halo outside the BH by means of an Einstein cluster. The frequencies then decrease by a similar amount, but the real part follows the Schwarzschild value multiplied by the gravitational redshift factor of the halo. A jump in the density at the inner edge of the halo scatters the waves and changes the damping rate by up to about $9\%$. For a continuous density profile, deviations from the redshift relation remain below about $1.5\%$ for $r_{\rm in}=3M$ and below about $0.5\%$ for the cases with $r_{\rm in}\geq4M$. No unstable modes are found within the perturbation sectors and parameter configurations examined.

Evidence for a nova super-remnant surrounding the recurrent nova V745 Scorpii using eROSITA

Michael W. Healy-Kalesh, Sara Saeedi, Manami Sasaki, Matthew J. Darnley, Michael M. Shara, Werner Becker, Martin G. F. Mayer, Federico Zangrandi

Nova super-remnants (NSRs) are vast shell-like structures encompassing recurrent novae (RNe), created through the excavation of large amounts of local interstellar medium by repeated nova eruptions. Simulations predict that such NSRs should surround all novae. Indeed, NSRs have now been discovered around the Galactic RNe KT Eridani, RS Ophiuchi and T Coronae Borealis, and the Large Magellanic Cloud RN LMCN 1971-08a, to place alongside the prototypical NSR in M31, associated with the extreme RN, M31N 2008-12a. Highly energetic novae ejecta impacting the inner boundary of the NSR shell alongside inter-ejecta shocks are predicted to generate high levels of X-ray emission in the form of an extended shell-like structure. A search for X-ray emission from a present NSR surrounding the Galactic RN, V745 Scorpii, using observations from the extended Roentgen Survey with an Imaging Telescope Array (eROSITA) on board Spektrum-Roentgen-Gamma (Spektr-RG, SRG) led to the identification of a shell coincident with the nova. Based on (i) the structure being aligned with the location of the central nova; (ii) its size being consistent with the sizes of other known NSRs; (iii) the similarity between the emission radial profiles of the X-ray structure and the confirmed NSR around M31N 2008-12a; (vi) the presence of a corresponding infrared cavity; (v) the resemblance of the radial profile of the synthetic X-ray ring derived from a modelled NSR structure with the radial profile of the observed ring; and (vi) the lack of other sources capable of creating such a large structure, we propose this X-ray structure to be another Galactic NSR to be uncovered, and the first NSR to be captured through its X-ray emission.

Probing the inner disk edge of rotating neutron stars with dipole excitations in finite nuclei

P. S. Koliogiannis, N. Paar

The nuclear equation of state shapes both the structure of rapidly rotating neutron stars and the orbital motion in their surrounding spacetime. Its uncertainty affects the mass and radius at which the innermost stable circular orbit reaches the stellar surface, a condition that defines the transition between disks whose inner edge is set by the surface and those truncated by orbital instability. We quantify how measurements of the electric dipole polarizability of neutron-rich nuclei reduce the uncertainty in this transition and in the orbital properties at the disk inner edge. We compute uniformly rotating neutron stars in general relativity for a family of equations of state that systematically vary the symmetry energy, derived from relativistic energy density functionals with density-dependent point-coupling interactions. We compare the results for the full family with those for the subset consistent with the measured dipole polarizabilities of finite nuclei. The dipole polarizability constraint reduces the spread in transition mass and equatorial radius by factors of about seven to ten across the spin frequencies considered. Along this transition, it also confines the orbital frequency at the disk inner edge to a band narrower than $20$~Hz at each spin. Combined with a measured neutron-star spin, a kilohertz quasi-periodic oscillation interpreted as disk orbital motion further restricts the mass--radius region, allowing an independent mass estimate to probe the symmetry-energy constraint. The dipole response of finite nuclei thus informs both the structure of rotating neutron stars and the orbital properties of their inner accretion disks.

Impact of Neutrino Trapping on the Equation of State and Structure of Hybrid Stars with 2SC Quark Matter

Andrea Sabatucci, Armen Sedrakian

We investigate how sensitive the effects of neutrino trapping on hot hybrid stars are to uncertainties in the hadronic and quark equations of state and in the thermodynamic construction of the hadron--quark transition. The hadronic phase is described by finite-temperature covariant density functional equations of state, and the quark phase by a three-flavor Nambu--Jona-Lasinio (NJL) model with repulsive vector interactions and two-flavor color superconductivity. Because confinement is absent from the NJL model, an effective pressure shift $B^*$ is used phenomenologically to locate the deconfinement transition. We construct isentropic stellar equations of state with and without trapped electron neutrinos, vary the quark vector coupling and the nuclear symmetry-energy slope, and compare global with local conservation of electron-lepton number within phase coexistence. Neutrino trapping generically shifts deconfinement toward higher density throughout the parameter range considered. Increasing the vector interaction stiffens quark matter and raises the maximum stellar mass, but only moderately for the couplings studied. We also examine stronger diquark pairing and RG-consistent regularization, instead of sharp momentum cut-off: both affect the pairing gap, phase boundaries, and quark-core composition more strongly than they alter the qualitative trends of the stellar mass--radius sequences. Conversely, trapping reduces the sensitivity to the hadronic symmetry-energy slope because the enhanced proton fraction drives matter toward greater isospin symmetry. Global and local lepton-number prescriptions produce rather similar mass--radius relations but substantially different internal phase structures: global conservation leads to a considerably more extended mixed phase.

Nonradial oscillation modes of axion condensed neutron stars in the Cowling approximation

Antonio Gómez-Bañón, Pantelis Pnigouras, José A. Pons

In light QCD axion models, finite-density corrections to the axion potential can source a nonvanishing axion field inside neutron stars. Extending our previous analysis of radial oscillations, we study the nonradial ($l=2$) oscillation spectrum of such axion condensed neutron stars in the relativistic Cowling approximation, keeping the fluid and axion perturbations fully coupled and dynamical. The condensate modifies the fluid spectrum in two ways: the altered equilibrium structure raises the $f$- and $p$-mode frequencies by up to $\sim20\%$, while the dynamical coupling to the axion field partially compensates this shift, almost entirely for the $f$-mode. Fluid modes with frequencies above the axion mass acquire a new damping channel through axion emission. The $f$-mode remains below this threshold for all configurations supporting a condensate, whereas the $p$-modes are damped on timescales that can be as short as $\sim10$ ms, shorter than their gravitational-wave damping times, except for certain axion masses where the outgoing axion amplitude nearly vanishes. The spectrum also contains a family of strongly damped axion-dominated quasinormal modes, with imaginary parts of order kHz, and a sequence of gravitationally bound axion states analogous to gravitational atom levels. Below the propagation threshold, the bound $p$-modes undergo avoided crossings with these levels, exchanging their fluid- and axion-dominated character.

$γ$-ray variability of changing-look blazars: FSRQ-like jet behaviour

Zahoor Malik, B. van Soelen, Sikandar Akbar, Zahir Shah

Changing-look blazars (CLBs) move between the flat-spectrum radio quasar (FSRQ) and BL Lacertae (BL Lac) optical classifications. Either the accretion flow switches regime, or a brightening jet continuum swamps the broad lines while the system itself is unchanged. The two pictures predict the same optical behaviour but differ in the jet, so the $γ$-ray emission could potentially separate them. Catalogue quantities averaged over the mission cannot, because a permanently intermediate source and one alternating between two states return the same mean. We therefore use 17.9\,yr of \textit{Fermi}-LAT light curves at 3-, 7- and 30-day cadence, comparing CLBs with BL Lac and FSRQ controls selected and analysed identically. CLBs and FSRQs have indistinguishable fractional variability, with medians of 0.483 and 0.437 at 30-day cadence ($p = 0.94$), while BL Lacs are far less variable at 0.262 ($p = 1.4\times10^{-4}$). The result survives restricting the controls to low-synchrotron-peaked sources and matching in flux and sampling. Several further diagnostics group CLBs with FSRQs, as does their Eddington-scaled disc luminosity. Followed through time, CLB light curves show no excess of bimodal flux distributions, and the direction of the optical transition does not predict how the $γ$-ray spectrum evolves. CLBs do reach hard, BL Lac-like spectra more often than FSRQs, but never for longer than chance allows. Only in this, and in how their spectra respond to brightening, do they fall between the two classes. The changing-look phenomenon therefore leaves little imprint on the $γ$-ray jet, favouring a variable jet continuum over a change of accretion regime.

The First Deep Learning all-sky List of Fermi-LAT Gamma-ray Sources

Saptashwa Bhattacharyya, Giacomo Principe, Dmitry Malyshev, Roberto Ruiz de Austri, Gabrijela Zaharijas, Sascha Caron, Fiorenzo Stoppa, Guðlaugur Johannesson

Aims: We develop a deep-learning-based pipeline to construct a list of γ-ray sources and to quantitatively assess the impact of background uncertainties on source detection and characterization. This work extends and enhances the existing AutoSource-ID algorithm, previously adopted on optical observations, for applications on γ-ray data. Methods: Source detection was performed through image segmentation using a U-Net architecture, while dedicated deep neural networks (DNNs) were employed for source localization, flux estimation, and the classification of detected sources as either genuine or spurious. The training data were generated from Monte Carlo simulations based on 10 years of Fermi-LAT observations. Results: We present the first Fermi-LAT γ-ray all-sky source list derived using a deep-learning-based method (1FDL), containing more than 3800 sources with estimated positions, fluxes, and probability scores that quantify the likelihood of each detection being genuine or spurious. We further evaluated the robustness of the pipeline against variations in the interstellar emission model (IEM) and compared the resulting source list with the 4FGL-DR2 catalog. Conclusion: We find that the our deep-learning-based approach achieves performance comparable to, or better than, the likelihood-based methods employed in the standard Fermi-LAT catalog pipeline when tested against changes in the IEM. The 1FDL source list is broadly consistent with the Fermi-LAT 4FGL-DR2 catalog obtained over the same 10-year observational period, including a few new sources confirmed in the recent Fermi-LAT 16-year source list (FL16Y). These results demonstrate the potential of DNN methods for the detection and characterization of γ-ray sources and provide a foundation for their application in future high-energy astrophysical analyses.

An SRG/eROSITA Study of Be X-ray binaries in Quiescence

Ajith Balu, Kinjal Roy, Biswajit Paul

Be X-ray binaries (BeXRBs) are mostly transient X-ray binaries with usually a neutron star as the compact object and a Be star as their optical companion. We performed a comprehensive analysis of BeXRBs in quiescence with the extended ROentgen Survey with an Imaging Telescope Array (eROSITA), the soft X-ray instrument on board the Spectrum-Roentgen-Gamma satellite, using its first all-sky survey (eRASS1) data. The transient BeXRBs spend majority of time in their quiescent state, but are often studied during their outbursts. A quiescence study is useful to probe the emission mechanism of BeXRBs at low mass accretion rates. The spectra of BeXRBs detected in the eRASS1 main catalogue could be well fitted with a powerlaw as well as a blackbody model. In the absence of detection of a BeXRB, we report the luminosity upper limit for its position in the sky. The X-ray luminosity for the quiescent BeXRBs detected with eROSITA have $L_{X}\sim10^{33-36}$ erg s$^{-1}$ for both absorbed powerlaw and blackbody models in most cases. For the absorbed blackbody model, the emission seems to come from a fraction of the neutron star's surface. The quiescence luminosity of BeXRBs are used to distinguish between different accretion models at low mass accretion rates, like accretion in the propeller regime or from a cold accretion disc. The detection space of BeXRBs in quiescence for the eRASS1 data is compared against previously reported luminosities across multiple X-ray observatories.

Coherent Radio Emission from Flux Rope Mergers in 3D Relativistic Reconnection

Jasmine Parsons, Lorenzo Sironi, Anatoly Spitkovsky, Alexander Chernoglazov

Coherent emission from merging plasmoids in relativistic reconnection is the leading model for radio emission originating in the outer magnetosphere of pulsars such as the Crab, and a candidate emission mechanism for fast radio bursts. This process has only been studied in 2D, and whether it survives in 3D, where the dynamics of structures in the reconnection layer can differ substantially, is still an open question. Here we present the first study of coherent wave generation from flux rope mergers in 3D relativistic reconnection. We use particle-in-cell simulations of relativistic reconnection with outflow boundary conditions and guide fields $B_g/B_0 = \{0,0.1,0.3\}$, and measure the efficiency $η$ with which inflowing magnetic energy is converted into escaping coherent waves. We find $η\sim 7\times10^{-4}$ without a guide field, a factor of a few below 2D results, and $η\sim(1\text{-}2) \times10^{-3}$ at moderate guide fields, comparable to 2D with no guide field. We propose a physical picture for why the efficiency remains comparable to 2D, based on how reconnected field is transported away from the diffusion regions. The escaping waves are linearly polarized, with a polarization angle set by the direction of the upstream field. In the presence of a guide field, waves escaping from opposite sides of the reconnection layer therefore differ in polarization angle by $ \sim 2\arctan(B_g/B_0)$. Our results support relativistic magnetic reconnection as the origin of coherent radio emission from Crab-like pulsars, and strengthen its case as a potential emission mechanism for fast radio bursts.

Searches for new particle physics by methods of classical and neutrino astronomy

Sergey Troitsky

Selected astrophysical methods for testing particle-physics models are reviewed. Stellar structure and evolution constrain additional channels of energy loss and transport; high-energy photon propagation probes photon mixing with new particles and modifications of electron-positron pair production; large-volume neutrino telescopes are used to search for magnetic monopoles, neutrinos from superheavy dark-matter decay, and non-standard neutrino propagation. Recent applications of these methods are discussed, involving the tip of the red-giant branch in the color-luminosity diagram, gamma-ray burst 221009A, and data from the Baikal Gigaton Volume Detector (Baikal-GVD) and the IceCube experiment. In each case, particle-physics parameters are inferred through a forward model that accounts for the response to new physics, propagation, detection, and nuisance parameters.

X-ray Emission Study of TeV Blazars

Zhihao Ouyang, Hubing Xiao, Shangchun Xie, Jingyu Wu, Xuhong Ye, Junhang Liu, Lili Yang, Marina Manganaro, Jianzhen Chen, Junhui Fan

In this work, we present a systematic X-ray study of 85 TeV-detected blazars based on archival \emph{Swift}-XRT and \emph{NuSTAR} observations available up to May 2024. We derive unabsorbed X-ray fluxes and photon indices through spectral fitting, and investigate their light curves, spectral energy distributions, flux distributions, power spectral densities, and flux-index correlations. Using the fractional variability method, we identify 78 sources as variable in the soft X-ray band and 14 sources as variable in the hard X-ray band. For well-sampled soft X-ray light curves, the flux distributions are generally better described by log-normal functions, while the PSD slopes span $β_{\rm PSD}=0.1$--$1.6$, with most values between 1.0 and 1.6, where the PSD is defined as $P(f)\propto f^{-β_{\rm PSD}}$. We find positive correlations between TeV and X-ray luminosities, supporting a leptonic emission scenario. We also find a correlation between X-ray and disk luminosities, whereas no significant correlation is found between X-ray luminosity and black hole mass.

Self lensing in BH-NS binaries as progenitors for periodically repeating Fast Radio Bursts

L. E. Rodríguez, M. Cruces, G. Voisin, J. Vera-Casanova, C. A. Braga

Fast radio bursts (FRBs) are bright extragalactic transients whose origin remains uncertain. The discovery of repeating FRBs, some with cyclic activity windows, opens new prospects for testing their progenitors. Neutron stars (NSs) are strong candidates, as they naturally emit radio pulses with temporal and spectral properties resembling those of FRBs, an interpretation reinforced by the detection of an FRB-like burst from the Galactic magnetar SGR 1935+2154. Nevertheless, the pulse energy of typical radio-emitting NSs remains several orders of magnitude below that of even the faintest observed FRBs. We explore a binary system in which a NS orbits a black hole (BH), so that NS radio pulses are gravitationally amplified through self-lensing, producing characteristic signatures in the energy distribution, activity windows, and waiting times that can be contrasted with observations. We implement a simple NS-BH binary model with a circular orbit undergoing Lense-Thirring precession, and compute how the pulses are lensed along the orbit, including pulse splitting and time delays. Pure Keplerian orbits produce activity windows several orders of magnitude shorter than those of periodic FRBs. Post-Keplerian orbits with Lense-Thirring precession are more promising, yielding windows of a few to tens of days. Lensing transforms an intrinsic log-normal NS pulse-energy distribution, producing a high-energy power-law tail spanning up to four orders of magnitude. Interpreting the observed millisecond waiting times between consecutive bursts as lensing-induced image delays, we infer BH masses of 10^3-10^4 solar masses, in the intermediate-mass (IMBH) range. However, the amplification in our model is insufficient to bridge the energy gap between Galactic magnetar radio pulses and FRBs. Moreover, the non-detection of NS-IMBH systems suggests a low abundance, so this channel is likely rare.

Discovery of a New Electron Spectrum Produced by Fast Cooling of Relativistic Electrons

Z. Lucas Uhm

In energetic astrophysical sources like the gamma-ray bursts (GRBs) and blazars, the relativistic electrons lose their energy efficiently through synchrotron radiation and inverse Compton (IC) scattering and can rapidly cool to energies below the minimum acceleration energy $γ_m$. It is widely known that such electrons develop a power-law spectrum $γ_e^{-2}$ below $γ_m$. However, this power-law spectrum is valid only if a steady-state population of electrons is assumed. In general, the cooled electrons below $γ_m$ evolve in a non-steady state and their spectrum deviates significantly from the power-law $γ_e^{-2}$. We present here a new electron spectrum $f_{\rm U}$ that describes this fast-cooled, generally-evolving, and non-steady-state population of electrons below $γ_m$. We consider two cooling cases: (1) when the electrons undergo radiative (i.e., synchrotron + IC) cooling, and (2) when the electrons experience both radiative and adiabatic cooling. We first derive $f_{\rm U}$ analytically and find an explicit expression of $f_{\rm U}$ for each cooling case. Secondly, we show that $f_{\rm U}$ is a solution to a Fokker-Planck equation in the entire energy range below $γ_m$ and derive a general version of $f_{\rm U}$ for each cooling case. We also compare $f_{\rm U}$ to a detailed numerical calculation of a blazar electron spectrum and find an excellent agreement for the whole energy range below $γ_m$. Thus, $f_{\rm U}$ gives us a general electron spectrum to describe the entire cooled electrons below $γ_m$. Our discovery of a new fast-cooling solution $f_{\rm U}$ below $γ_m$ may find it applicable in a wide range of energetic phenomena in high-energy astrophysics.

Quantum Annealing for Gamma-ray Source Detection and Localization with AGILE Data

Alessandro Rizzo, Nicolò Parmiggiani, Andrea Bulgarelli, Gabriele Panebianco, Carlo Burigana, Vincenzo Fabrizio Cardone, Farida Farsian, Massimo Meneghetti, Giuseppe Murante, Giuseppe Sarracino, Roberto Scaramella, Francesco Schillirò, Vincenzo Testa, Tiziana Trombetti, Fabio Roberto Vitello

The detection and localization of gamma-ray sources via maximum-likelihood analysis is a complex parameter optimization problem. We address this challenge using quantum annealing, a computational approach that solves optimization tasks by encoding them into the ground state of an Ising Hamiltonian. Specifically, we apply this method to observations from AGILE's Gamma-Ray Imaging Detector (GRID) in the 30 MeV-50 GeV band. We reformulated the standard analysis as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) problem. We tested this method on a set of AGILE observations generated with AGILEpy, solving the QUBO with simulated quantum annealing. The method reproduces the classical flux and position estimates within their statistical uncertainty and identifies the correct grid node among the candidate positions in 97% of the maps. These promising results demonstrate that quantum annealing could be used in this optimization problem, laying the ground for its deployment on actual quantum annealers.

FLITS: an interactive, reproducible workbench for fast radio burst analysis

Dirk Kuiper, Ziggy Pleunis, Jason W. T. Hessels

Fast radio bursts (FRBs) are brief flashes of radio emission whose time, frequency, and polarization structure constrain their sources and propagation environments. FLITS (Fast-Look Interactive Transient Suite) is an open-source Python package with a browser interface for analysing detected bursts. It combines data inspection, interference mitigation, measurement, and modelling, including fits of the fitburst burst model, in a shared session, and reads SIGPROC filterbank, PSRFITS, and CHIME/FRB HDF5 data. The same routines measure larger samples from the command line and flag bursts whose automatic selections need checking. Trial dispersion measures can be scored by signal-to-noise, by burst structure, or with a smoothed-structure metric that also reports the range of dispersion measures consistent with the best one. Portable session files retain the selected data regions, excluded channels, calibration inputs, available results, input-data checksum, and software provenance, and supported measurements can be replayed from the command line and compared with their saved values. Uncertainty labels identify missing calibration information. A public reference workflow provides downloadable data and numerical checks.

History of Turn-Ons - the irregular 35-day clock - of Hercules X-1

Ruediger Staubert, Konstantin Postnov, Vladislav Revnivtsev, Lorenzo Ducci

Hercules X-1 and Centaurus X-3 are the first ever detected - and as such identified - binary X-ray pulsars. Here we concern ourselves with the 35-day flux modulation of Her X-1, immediately found at its discovery, together with the binary period of 1.7 days and the pulsation with 1.24 s. The 35-day flux modulation shows two flux maxima, the so-called Main-On and the Short-On (with reduced flux). As noticed early on, the period, or the length of the cycle, is variable. The system constitutes an irregular clock, with a more or less stable mean period of near 34.85 d. We consider the repeated rapid increases of the flux from the off-state to the Main-On (the socalled Turn-Ons) as the best timing marker to study this clock. Here we present a complete list of Turn-On times over 55 years, from the discovery of Her X-1 to today (2026), based on historic work from the literature and on observational data provided by five monitoring instruments. We identify to following basic features of the irregular 35-day clock: The Turn-Ons show preference to occur at orbital phases 0.25 and 0.75. The duration of the 35-day cycle clusters around three discrete values - 20.0, 20.5 and 21.0 orbital cycles with a dominant peak at 20.5 Porb (57%). The long-term trend in the 35-day period duration is seen in the O-C (Observed minus Calculated) diagram. There are two sections, separated by the long Anomalous Low of 1999/200 (AL3), which can be modeled by parabolic functions with a smooth evolution of the 35-day period. A medium-term variation is observed in the form of Anomalous Lows, an essential shut-off (at least of soft) X-rays. We find an apparent underlying period of 5.4 yr. There are short-term (hundreds of days) variations (in part quasi-periodic) overlaid to the long-term trend. Straight ups and downs in the O-C diagram, demonstrating rather rapid 35 day period changes.

Quasiparticle Modeling of Strange Quark Stars with In-Medium QCD Effects at Finite Temperature and Strong Magnetic Fields

Yogesh Kumar, Mihir Arora, Manas Lohani, Poonam Jain, Pargin Bangotra, Rajeev

This work investigates the properties of spin polarized strange quark matter SQM and the resulting structure of strange quark stars SQSs within a thermodynamically self consistent quasiparticle framework. By developing a dynamic bag constant intrinsically coupled to the medium dependent reduction of quark effective masses we ensure strict internal thermodynamic consistency. This coupling gives rise to a distinct non monotonic behavior in the speed of sound significantly stiffening the Equation of State EoS at high densities. Consequently our model predicts maximum stable gravitational masses up to 2.19 Mo. These results demonstrate that hot magnetized SQM provides a robust and viable mechanism to explain ultra massive pulsars such as PSR J0740 6620 and PSR J0952 0607 without requiring unrealistic thermal or structural assumptions.

A microscopic analysis of shear acceleration in the relativistic regime

Sophie Aerdker, Xingwei Gong, Martin Lemoine, Frank Rieger

Relativistic shear flows, as encountered in jets of powerful astrophysical sources, offer promising sites of particle acceleration up to the highest energies. This stochastic acceleration process is usually described with Fokker-Planck transport equations, which assume small momentum gains per scattering. The present paper introduces a covariant microscopic description of relativistic shear acceleration, building on the formalism of generalized Fermi acceleration and formulating angular scattering in the locally comoving frame in terms of stochastic differential equations. This allows us to model relativistic shear profiles and to study the transition from gradual to non-gradual shear acceleration, where the particle mean free path becomes comparable to the shear length scale. Assuming an infinite shear layer, linear in the flow four-velocity, we observe that the shear rate peaks where the flow is mildly relativistic, whereas Lorentz aberration suppresses the transverse velocity of particles where the flow is relativistic. Consequently, particles that leave the central layer experience long waiting times with a heavy-tailed, power-law distribution. The momentum evolution becomes subdiffusive and the acceleration time can substantially exceed local estimates. We successfully reproduce the Monte Carlo energy distributions using a two-state continuous-time random walk with Lévy-distributed waiting times. We also show how a momentum-dependent mean-free path can introduce breaks in the spectrum and extend our discussion to finite jet profiles. These effects should be accounted for in models of relativistic jets as sources of high-energy particles.

2:1 Harmonics in the Radio Light Curves of Supermassive Black Hole Binary Candidates

A. C. S. Readhead, M. F. Aller, R. D. Blandford, T. Surti, P. V. De la Parra, A. G. Sullivan, P. Mróz, N. Murray, M. C. Begelman, B. Molina, M. L. Lister, E. R. Most, S. O'Neill, V. A. Diamantopoulos, N. Globus, T. Hovatta, I. Liodakis, T. J. Pearson, S. E. Tremblay

We present evidence of 2:1 harmonic periodicities in two supermassive black hole binary (SMBHB) candidates (PKS J0805-0111 and PKS 2131-021) in the Owens Valley Radio Observatory (OVRO) + University of Michigan Radio Astronomical Observatory (UMRAO) monitoring programs. This potential discovery follows the previous detection of a 2:1 harmonic periodicity in the SMBHB candidate J1309+1154 from these programs. If confirmed, these suggest a physical origin of blazars exhibiting strong periodicities favoring 2:1 commensurabilities in their light curves. There are significant differences between the 2:1 harmonics in these three blazars, which may be due to differences in their central engines. These three SMBHB candidates demonstrate the importance of careful dissection, i.e. cutting apart and close examination, of blazar light curves, to reveal underlying evidence of coherence between different aspects of the periodicities observed in blazars in the presence of correlated random noise variations. This is particularly important for blazar SMBHB searches, given that the observed periods thus far are all longer than 2 years, and can disappear for long timespans. Therefore confirming evidence, such as harmonics, may be crucial in confirming the reality of periodicities, and identifying blazar SMBHB candidates. We present four models that can account for 2:1 commensurable periodicities in blazar light curves, together with explanations for why, on these models, shorter period SMBHB candidates have not thus far been observed, but might be anticipated, or not, in the future, depending on the model, thereby making these models discriminatory. Arguments pro and con the reality of these periodicities are presented.

Three-flavor neutrino Boltzmann transport and flavor instabilities in long-lived neutron-star merger remnants

Harry Ho-Yin Ng, Jiabao Liu, Arthur Offermans, Shoichi Yamada, Elias R. Most, Luciano Rezzolla

Binary neutron-star (BNS) mergers are multimessenger sources whose kilonova emission and nucleosynthesis depend sensitively on neutrino microphysics and flavor conversion. Most BNS merger simulations assume $μ$-$τ$ neutrino degeneracy in a two-flavor treatment. We investigate the fast flavor instability (FFI) and the collisional flavor instability (CFI) in long-lived BNS merger remnants using axisymmetric general-relativistic three-flavor Boltzmann neutrino transport with a comprehensive treatment of muons and muonic interactions, following a handoff from 3D grey moment-based simulations. Our simulations largely reproduce the parent 3D simulations, recovering trapped weak equilibrium in the core, muonic non-equilibrium in the semitransparent outer disk due to prolonged demuonization, and cold weak equilibrium in transparent regions. Linear stability analysis reveals strong and widespread CFI in all three flavor sectors, including narrow resonance-like structures. Muonic interactions reshape the FFI distribution, shifting the $eτ$-sector instability toward denser disk regions compared with the extended FFI outside the disk in the two-flavor model, while disk demuonization produces a strong $ν_μ$ excess and one-sided muon-lepton-number angular distributions that suppress FFI in the $eμ$ and $μτ$ sectors. Local three-flavor quantum kinetic equation (QKE) calculations reveal nonlinear coupling among flavor sectors that excites coherence even in an initially stable sector. Diagonal collisional repopulation is decisive for the nonlinear evolution and asymptotic flavor populations, and neglecting it overestimates flavor equilibration. We establish the necessity of three-flavor transport from both classical and quantum perspectives, and underscore the importance of consistently coupling flavor conversion with diagonal collisional repopulation in future CFI subgrid models.

Periodic radio emission from a rapidly rotating T8 ultracool dwarf

Iris de Ruiter, Joshua Pritchard, Tara Murphy, Andrew K. Hughes, David L. Kaplan, Kovi Rose, Andrew Zic

We report the discovery of periodic radio bursts from the T8 brown dwarf WISE J151721.13+052929.3 (WISE J1517+05), detected with ASKAP and MeerKAT. The emission is characterised by a stable periodicity of $1.149 \pm 0.002$ hr, placing WISE J1517+05 among the fastest rotating radio-detected ultracool dwarfs (UCDs). The radio bursts are broadband and highly variable, reaching a peak spectral luminosity of $L_ν = 5.6 \times 10^{15}\,\mathrm{erg\,s^{-1}\,Hz^{-1}}$, making WISE J1517+05 the brightest periodic UCD to date. The high brightness temperature of the radio bursts ($T_B>10^{11}$ K), combined with the rotational modulation, strongly favour an auroral electron cyclotron maser emission (ECME) origin. In contrast to most known radio-bursting UCDs, the bursts exhibit relatively low and variable circular polarisation fractions (typically $\sim 20\%$). These properties suggest significant depolarisation of intrinsically highly polarised ECME, likely due to propagation effects in a structured magnetospheric plasma. Notably, we also observe a polarisation reversal within a single burst profile, which is currently unexplained. We model the emission using a dipolar magnetic field with emission arising from a single active field line, and find a moderate inclination angle of $i = 47.9^{+11.4}_{-9.2}$ degrees and a magnetic obliquity of $β= 40.1^{+9.2}_{-10.9}$ degrees. WISE J1517+05 expands the parameter space of radio-active UCDs toward higher luminosities and lower polarisation fractions, suggesting that current surveys may be sensitive to a larger population than previously recognised, while searches relying on methods that assume high circular polarisation fractions risk missing a subset of UCDs. More broadly, this discovery reinforces the idea that rapid rotation plays a key role in generating strong dipolar magnetic fields in UCDs that can give rise to auroral radio emission.

Quark Saturation in Magnetized Pure Neutron Matter

Toru Kojo

We study how magnetic fields affect the onset of quark saturation, which limits the domain of applicability of a purely baryonic description. We consider matter composed of electrically neutral $ddu$ baryons, including neutron- and $Δ^0$-like states. Using a simple constituent-quark model, we compute the baryon wave functions and spectra in magnetic fields. The magnetic field polarizes the spin-flavor structure, compresses the internal wave function in the transverse direction, and strongly suppresses the transverse kinetic energy. From the baryon wave functions, we construct the one-body reduced density matrix and derive the corresponding single-quark distributions in longitudinal momentum and Landau-level space. We then use these distributions to evaluate quark occupation probabilities in an ideal baryon gas and determine the onset of quark saturation. At finite magnetic field in the $+z$-direction, the $d_\downarrow$ quark in the lowest Landau level reaches saturation first. The resulting saturation density depends nonmonotonically on the magnetic-field strength: it decreases at weak fields and increases at stronger fields. This behavior reflects the competition between spin-flavor polarization, which promotes saturation, and transverse spatial squeezing, which delays saturation by broadening of the quark transverse momentum distribution.

Reconstruction of very inclined air showers with radio data and application to the first GRANDProto300 cosmic-ray candidates

Marion Guelfand, Valentin Decoene, Olivier Martineau, Simon Prunet, Sebastian Castro-Isern, Nathan Lebas

Radio detection of very inclined extensive air showers (EAS) offers a promising way to study ultra-high-energy cosmic rays (UHECRs) and neutrinos above $10^{17}$ eV. We present a method to reconstruct the arrival direction and energy of EAS with zenith angles above $60^\circ$, applicable in principle to upward-going neutrino-induced showers. The approach combines a point-source-like description of the radio wavefront with the Angular Distribution Function (ADF), a phenomenological model describing the angular pattern of electric field amplitudes in the 50-200 MHz band. On realistic cosmic-ray simulations, we achieve better than $0.07^\circ$ resolution on arrival direction and ~15\% on primary energy. Applied directly to voltage traces, this method allows efficient cosmic-ray selection with accurate direction reconstruction and first-order energy estimation. We illustrate the method on the first cosmic-ray candidates detected by the GRANDProto300 prototype of GRAND, validating its detection principle and paving the way for high-precision neutrino searches.

On the Interpretation of Tests of the Horizon Area Law

Selma Vangstein, Christopher J. Moore

This Letter investigates gravitational-wave tests of the horizon area law. Although they provide a striking consistency check of general relativity (GR), these tests rely on models that assume GR, making their interpretation inherently model dependent. We show with a specific example---simulations of merging boson stars---that exotic compact objects can mimic merging black holes and pass an area-law test at a confidence as high as $\sim 92\%$ despite not even having event horizons.

Dynamical constraints on the companion mass from pulsar timing and cyclotron line confirmation in the SFXT IGR J16418-4532

Ashwin Devaraj, Rahul Sharma, Ketan Rikame, Biswajit Paul

Supergiant Fast X-ray Transients (SFXTs) exhibit extreme X-ray variability on short timescales of minutes to hours, driven by accretion processes that are still poorly understood. IGR J16418–4532 is an intermediate SFXT in which X-ray pulsations have been detected and which has been proposed as a candidate cyclotron line source, making it a key system for probing neutron-star accretion physics and magnetic field properties in this class of sources. We aim to constrain the projected semi-major axis of the SFXT IGR J16418--4532, through timing analysis, and confirm the presence of a Cyclotron Resonant Scattering Feature (CRSF), thereby characterising the nature of the compact object in this system. We analysed a 148 ks long $\textit{NuSTAR}$ observation and several archival $\textit{RXTE}$ observations using epoch-folding techniques to search for pulsations while correcting the photon arrival times for a range of $a_x \sin(i)$ values. We performed phase-averaged spectroscopy using several different continuum models and assessed the significance of the cyclotron line feature. We detect coherent pulsations with $P_{\mathrm{spin}} = 1208.67 \pm 0.04$ s and $P_{\mathrm{spin}} = 1209.4178 \pm 0.0084$ s in $\textit{NuSTAR}$ and $\textit{RXTE/PCA}$ light curves, respectively. The pulsations exhibit clear dependence on the assumed projected semi-major axis, and are detected with the highest significance for $a_x\sin i = 55.1 \pm 3.5$ lt-s $(\textit{NuSTAR})$ and $51.3 \pm 2.6$ lt-s $(\textit{RXTE/PCA})$. The light curves show several flares, and the pulse profiles are found to evolve over the course of the observation. Sliding window pulse profiles reveal the pronounced effect of the orbital delay on the pulse arrival times in uncorrected data, which is fully removed post-orbital correction. Using the inferred orbital parameters, we constrain the mass and radius of the companion star to be in the range of $\sim$12--18 $M_\odot$ and $\sim$13--16 $R_\odot$, respectively. We report the first successful constraint on the binary mass function of an SFXT obtained by considering the orbital motion of the neutron star. Spectral analysis reveals a robust cyclotron absorption line at $\sim$50 keV, independent of the choice of the continuum, suggesting a magnetic field strength of $\sim$5 $\times 10^{12}$~G. The cyclotron line confirmation and dynamical constraints on the companion's mass indicate that IGR J16418--4532 hosts a neutron star with a magnetic field of B$\approx 5 \times 10^12 G$ accreting from a $\sim$12--18 $M_\odot$ companion. We demonstrate that optimising the pulse detection by correcting the photon arrival times with trial $a_x \sin(i)$ values rather than modelling the pulse arrival time delays offers a route to obtain dynamical mass measurements in other SFXTs whose variable pulse profiles limit the scope of conventional timing techniques.

Phase-Twist Instability in a Coprecessing Neutrino Plasma

Damiano F. G. Fiorillo, Dwaipayan Mukherjee, Basudeb Dasgupta, Georg G. Raffelt

Dense neutrino plasmas support collective flavor motion. We derive the first explicit dispersion relation about a flavor-nondiagonal background, focusing on the coprecession solution underlying spectral-split formation during the prompt neutrino burst of an electron-capture supernova. We show that slow phase modulations are unstable Goldstone modes, causing the coprecession plane to wobble with increasing amplitude. The instability emerges in the hydrodynamic limit, governed by conservation laws and without resolving flavor-precession resonances. Coprecessing neutrino plasmas resemble magnetized plasmas: a coherent flavor field drives flavor isospins to precess, and the phase-twist instability is the flavor analog of the firehose instability.

CROMA: a GPU-accelerated Fokker--Planck solver for modeling non-thermal emission in post-processing cosmological simulations

Kosuke Nishiwaki, Claudio Gheller, Gianfranco Brunetti, Katsuaki Asano

Numerical modeling of cosmic-ray (CR) spectra and the resulting non-thermal emission is important for understanding the physical mechanisms of CR acceleration and transport in astrophysical environments. Galaxy clusters and cosmic filaments provide particularly important environments for studying these processes, where shocks, turbulence, magnetic fields, and CRs jointly shape diffuse synchrotron emission. Spectrally resolved modeling of CRs, including cooling, re-acceleration, and hadronic interactions, requires solving time-dependent Fokker--Planck (FP) equations. An increasing number of studies employ FP solvers coupled with magnetohydrodynamical (MHD) simulations to follow the evolution of CRs in dynamically evolving environments. We present a new C/C++-based FP solver with GPU acceleration for post-processing outputs from cosmological MHD simulations. The code evolves CR proton and electron spectra along Lagrangian tracer trajectories and consistently includes re-acceleration and secondary-particle production processes. With the Chang--Cooper finite-difference scheme, the FP equation for each tracer is reduced to an independent tridiagonal system, which is solved on NVIDIA GPUs using a CUDA implementation of the parallel cyclic reduction (PCR) algorithm. The code uses sub-stepping between tracer snapshots, with the timestep constrained by the relevant CR cooling or re-acceleration timescale, and a batching algorithm that groups tracers with similar sub-stepping patterns for efficient execution. We find that the GPU acceleration increases the per-node throughput by a factor of $\approx$10 on the Leonardo Booster system operated by CINECA. The fast FP solver is useful for high-resolution simulations dedicated to comparison with next-generation radio observations and future multi-messenger applications combining radio, gamma-ray, and high-energy neutrino observations.

Semi-agnostic inspiral modeling: the massless scalar case at leading post-Newtonian order

Dario Rossi, Leonardo Gualtieri, Thomas P. Sotiriou

We introduce a semi-agnostic modeling framework for black hole binary inspirals beyond general relativity that fixes the field content without selecting a particular theory. We expand in the post-Newtonian order and in powers of $l/L$, where $l$ is the characteristic length-scale that controls deviations from General Relativity and $L$ is the characteristic length-scale of the system. We focus on the case of a massless scalar field coupled with the metric, at leading post-Newtonian order. Taking into account the scaling with $l/L$ of both the interaction terms and the quantities that characterise the sources, we find that the inspiral can be described in terms of a small number of universal parameters. Such a parametrisation is suitable for obtaining combined bounds from multiple sources and performing searches for specific fields.

Interaction of Strong X-mode Waves with Magnetized Pair Plasmas

Navin Sridhar

The interaction of strong ($a_0>1$), superluminal X-mode electromagnetic waves with magnetized pair plasma falls into two regimes set by $\varepsilon_{\rm B}\equiv a_0ω_{\rm B}/ω_0$, the ratio of the wave strength parameter to its frequency, in units of cyclotron frequency. For $\varepsilon_{\rm B}<1$ the wave propagates until induced scattering disrupts it. Using analytical theory and kinetic simulations, we show that the background magnetic field introduces a restoring frequency $Ω_{\rm B}$ to scattering; when $Ω_{\rm B}$ exceeds the scattering rate, the unattenuated length grows from $\simeq\varepsilon_{\rm p}^{-2/3}$ to $\simeq\varepsilon_{\rm B}^{1/2}/\varepsilon_{\rm p}$ wavelengths ($\varepsilon_{\rm p}\equiv a_0ω_{\rm p}/ω_0$; $ω_{\rm p}$ is the plasma frequency). When $a_0^2>\varepsilon_{\rm B}>1$, the plasma current is nonlinear, and the wave is damped while riding a motional electric field, so one half-cycle appears attenuated. This framework has implications for the duration of fast radio bursts and whether they can escape their surroundings.

Tidal contributions to neutron star orbital precession around Sgr $A^*$

H Lalrinfela, P. C. Lalremruati, Zodinmawia, N Malsawmtluangi

We investigate tidal contributions to the orbital precession of neutron stars around the supermassive black hole Sgr A*, using stellar configurations described by the Tolman - Oppenheimer - Volkoff equations. We examine the dependence of tidal precession on stellar compactness, pericentre distance, and the quadrupolar tidal Love number $k_2$, and compare its magnitude with general relativistic orbital precession. The calculated tidal angular shift decreases strongly with increasing pericentre distance and, for the stellar configurations considered, with increasing compactness. Larger Love numbers enhance the tidal contribution. This behaviour reflects the strong dependence of tidal precession on the stellar-radius-to-pericentre-distance ratio, $(R/r_p)^5$. Despite the enhancement during closer encounters, the predicted tidal shifts remain extremely small, reaching approximately $3.5 \times 10^{-18},μas$ within the displayed parameter range. The ratio of tidal to general relativistic precession spans approximately $10^{-33}$ - $10^{-28}$, indicating that relativistic precession overwhelmingly dominates. Our results suggest that direct detection of the neutron-star tidal contribution through orbital precession around Sgr A* would be exceptionally challenging under the assumptions of the adopted model.

The long-term evolution of a black hole accretion torus: a comparison between GRMHD simulations and relativistic thin disk theory

Andrew Mummery, Minghao Guo, Eliot Quataert, James M. Stone

In this paper we analyse the long-term evolution of a black hole accretion torus using novel GRMHD simulations which, for the first time, have been run out to extremely large evolutionary times $t_{\rm max} = 10^7 GM_\bullet/c^3$. This allows for detailed comparisons between the gross evolution of this GRMHD simulation and existing "spreading disk" analytical theories. We show that the long-term features of this evolution are well described by General Relativistic Thin Disk Theory (GRTDT), which reproduces various aspects of the GRMHD evolution to within a factor $\sim 2$, despite the disk in our simulation not being remotely thin, showing dynamically dominant (MAD) magnetic fields, and launching a powerful wind. Thin disk theory functions as an accurate and cheap effective theory for the evolution in radius and time of the bulk of the disk material, and for the time dependence of the horizon accretion rate. We argue that this effectiveness originates from the global conservation laws of mass and angular momentum, together with a turbulent stress which we find to be local and independent of density, lending support to the ad-hoc local-stress-tensor phenomenology employed in analytical models. We also find that the turbulent accretion rate variability in the inner disk is log-normally distributed, consistent with a key assumption in the modeling of observed variability.
