Three subpopulations of binary black holes in mass, spin magnitudes and tilts following GWTC-5

Asad Hussain, Maximiliano Isi, Aaron Zimmerman

The mass and spin distributions of merging binary black holes offer clues to their formation pathways, which remain uncertain. In this work, we model the population of binary black holes using generic subpopulations with distinct mass-ratio, spin-magnitude, and spin-tilt distributions, whose relative contributions vary with primary mass. Using the 259 binary black holes in the fifth LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA catalog (GWTC-5), we resolve three subpopulations with low, medium, and high spins. The low-spin population dominates for primary masses near $35 M_\odot$, contributes roughly half of the binaries for primary masses of $10 M_\odot$, and favors nearly equal mass ratios overall; its spins are consistent with both black holes having negligible spin, with a log Bayes factor of $\log_{10}B =6.1^{+0.2}_{-0.3}$ in favor of vanishing spins. The medium-spin population accounts for the other half of the population around primary masses of $10 M_\odot$ and favors spin magnitudes $χ_i \approx 0.2$ for both components, preferentially aligned orientations, and comparable mass ratios. The high-spin population dominates above primary masses of $60 M_\odot$ and also contributes at lower masses $\sim 20 M_\odot$; it favors a rapidly spinning primary ($χ_1 \approx 0.7$), a broad range of secondary spins, widely distributed spin orientations, and a broad mass-ratio distribution. Allowing for a fourth subpopulation yields no distinct additional mass-ratio or spin distribution, as it becomes degenerate with existing features. To resolve highly concentrated features in spin, we control the variance of standard Monte Carlo likelihood estimators using truncated Gaussian mixture models. Taken together, our results add to a growing body of evidence for substructure in the population of LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA black holes, providing further clues to uncover their formation histories.

A Population Synthesis Model for Odd Radio Circles Produced by Shock-Bubble Interactions

Yiting Wang, Sebastian Heinz, Alexander Tchekhovskoy

Odd Radio Circles (ORCs) are edge-brightened rings of diffuse radio emission of roughly arcminute size whose physical origin remains debated. One proposed mechanism is vortex formation through Shock-Bubble interaction (SBI), in which a merger or accretion shock re-energizes a fossil radio lobe into an edge-brightened ring. We test whether this mechanism can reproduce the observed ORC abundance using a Monte Carlo population-synthesis model that combines the galaxy stellar mass function, the radio-AGN incidence relation, dark-matter halo merger histories, and the inflation and buoyant survival of AGN-inflated bubbles, with synthetic radio fluxes and ring geometries drawn from a grid of magnetohydrodynamic SBI simulations. Applying survey-specific selection to the ASKAP-EMU Pilot Survey (944 MHz) and the LOFAR Two-metre Sky Survey (LoTSS; 144 MHz), we predict $2.7^{+3.9}_{-1.6}$ detectable ORCs in the EMU Pilot, matching the three found there, and $268^{+331}_{-148}$ in LoTSS, of which $7.5^{+9.5}_{-4.2}$ have a counterpart at 1.4 GHz. The predicted hosts are mostly central galaxies in group-scale halos. The predicted rings are physically smaller and lie at lower redshifts than the galaxies identified as hosts of the known ORCs, suggesting that SBI produces mainly the nearby part of the ORC population, or that some ORCs are associated with the wrong host. The population synthesis model also predicts a large population of low surface brightness steep-spectrum ORCs in the LoTSS band, implying a large hidden population and predicting that deeper low-frequency surveys should uncover many more ORCs than are currently known. These results indicate that shock-bubble interactions can account for at least part of the observed incidence of ORCs.

GRB 260924A: Extreme Energetics, Bright GeV Emission, and Exceptional X-ray and Optical Luminosity

Nissim Fraija, Peter Veres, Rosa L. Becerra, Antonio Galván-Gámez, Thomas Freeman, Simone Dichiara, Maria G Dainotti

We present a multiwavelength study of GRB~260924A at $z=3.51\pm0.10$ using \textit{Fermi}, \textit{Swift}, and ground-based optical and near-infrared observations. Its isotropic-equivalent gamma-ray energy, $E_{γ,\mathrm{iso}}\simeq9.79\times10^{54}$~erg, places it among the most energetic GRBs observed. The prompt emission comprised two bright episodes separated by weaker activity. The X-ray afterglow ranked among the most luminous in our comparison sample at selected post-flare epochs, with a luminosity comparable to that of GRB~221009A at a rest-frame time of 3.5~ks. At a common redshift of $z=1$, the optical afterglow outshone GRB~221009A by approximately 2~mag at 0.3~d and 1~mag near 1~d. LAT emission extended to approximately 1100~s and showed an early excess above the subsequent decay. The highest-energy photon, 43.3~GeV, arrived at 82~s during the first episode; a 14.2~GeV photon arrived at 304~s between episodes. We describe the afterglow light curves through forward-shock synchrotron and synchrotron self-Compton (SSC) emission, with reverse-shock SSC accounting for the early LAT excess. We connect the early and late afterglow phases by modelling the X-ray rebrightening at several kiloseconds through energy injection from trailing ejecta and evolving shock microphysics. The late afterglow suggests a stratified medium, modelled as $n(R)\propto R^{-1.2}$. Within this interpretation, the magnetic contrast $R_B\equiv(\varepsilon_{B,r}/\varepsilon_{B,f})^{1/2} \simeq2.6$ suggests magnetized ejecta, while the highest-energy LAT photons exceed the predicted synchrotron limit, supporting an SSC contribution.

The Galactic Center Gamma-Ray Excess and the Look-Elsewhere Effect

Isabel S. Sands, Philip F. Hopkins

The origin of the excess of GeV gamma-rays in the Milky Way's galactic center observed by Fermi-LAT has been an open question for nearly two decades. The two leading interpretations of this signal are that it is produced either by annihilating dark matter (DM), or arises from unresolved point sources, such as a novel population of millisecond pulsars. In both scenarios, uncertainties and limitations in the modeling of galactic diffuse gamma-ray emission, such as the steady-state approximation of cosmic ray (CR) propagation and assumption of an axisymmetric distribution of CRs, complicate the interpretation of the galactic center excess (GCE). Simulations with full magnetohydrodynamics (MHD) and CR transport can provide a new avenue for understanding the astrophysical phenomena that drive galactic diffuse gamma-ray emission. We utilize MHD simulations of Milky Way analogs with time-dependent, spectrally-resolved CR transport to reconstruct diffuse gamma-ray emission. Comparing instantaneous emission to a time-averaged baseline, we find residuals up to several times larger than the Fermi GCE, with a wide range of spectral shapes and spatial morphologies. At some simulation snapshots, the spectrum and morphology of the residuals resemble the Fermi GCE. Our results suggest that the Fermi GCE may be a manifestation of the look-elsewhere effect- a transient fluctuation in diffuse emission from stellar sources, observed at an inopportune moment- and motivate the development of new Fermi-LAT analysis templates that account for time-dependent CR transport.

Probing the star-forming environment of GRB across cosmic ages with high-resolution X-ray spectroscopy: From \textit{XMM-Newton}/RGS and \textit{XRISM}/Resolve to \textit{NewAthena}/X-IFU

Luigi Piro, Aishwarya Linesh Thakur, Alfredo Luminari, Fabrizio Nicastro, Yair Krongold

Optical and infrared GRB absorption spectroscopy provides valuable insight into host-galaxy properties across cosmic time. However, the star-forming region embedding the progenitor, on scales $\lesssim 100~\mathrm{pc}$ is heavily ionized by the GRB emission, making X-ray spectroscopy the sole probe of its physical and chemical conditions. We characterize the GRB-embedding medium as a function of progenitor type and cosmic epoch via high-resolution X-ray spectroscopy. We extend our study to high-redshift GRBs to evaluate how effectively X-ray absorption signatures can distinguish between pristine, Pop~III-, and Pop~II-enriched environments. Using the time-dependent photoionization code \texttt{TEPID}, we self-consistently calculate the ionization structure of the absorber, the energies and the strengths of the resulting absorption lines. We analyze a flux-selected sample of GRBs showing strong evidence for time-dependent photoionization, and search for predicted narrow features in the RGS spectra. We also assess the performance of \textit{XRISM}/Resolve and \textit{NewAthena}/X-IFU in detecting these features. In the single archival case with sufficient RGS signal-to-noise ratio (GRB~090618 at $z=0.54$), we find $\approx 3σ$ combined evidence for narrow, highly ionized Fe~L absorption lines. For bright nearby GRBs, \textit{XRISM}/Resolve can detect Fe~K absorption lines even in its current gate-valve-closed configuration ($\sim 3$ expected events/yr). \textit{NewAthena}/X-IFU will enable systematic population studies of GRB environments, delivering redshifts with precision comparable to optical/IR spectroscopy, unaffected by dust obscuration and Ly-$α$ forest absorption. Crucially, it will precisely diagnose the density, size, metallicity, and abundance patterns, ultimately revealing whether high-$z$ GRBs originate in pristine, Pop~III-, or Pop~II-enriched media.

Tracking the Evolution of Supermassive Black Hole Binaries with Pulsar Terms

Bence Bécsy, Magdalena Siwek

Pulsar timing arrays will soon be able to resolve the loudest supermassive black hole binaries contributing to the stochastic gravitational wave background at nanohertz frequencies. While the few decades of observations are typically not enough to see these binaries evolve in frequency, the so-called pulsar terms offer a glimpse into the past and can help us map out the frequency evolution of these binaries over tens of thousands of years. Measuring this long-term frequency evolution can allow us to look for deviations from a vacuum binary evolution due to various environmental effects. We develop a Fisher-matrix approach to calculate how precisely the frequency can be tracked over time in this manner, and demonstrate its use on a realistic near-future pulsar timing array with a 30-year baseline. We find that the environmental effects predicted by 2-dimensional hydrodynamic simulations of circumbinary disks are unlikely to be detectable in the circular binaries we considered, however, in some cases they might result in biased chirp mass recovery if ignored. We also demonstrate how this approach allows one to test the orbital evolution predicted by general relativity in a purely gravitational-wave driven inspiral, and to put upper limits on additional generic environmental effects described by a power-law hardening model.

Magnetic Reconnection Modifies Cosmic-Ray Transport in the Interstellar Medium

Antoine Baldacchino-Jordan, Michal Hanasz, Vinod Pisharody, Dominik Wóltański, Artur Gawryszczak

We present numerical magnetohydrodynamic (MHD) simulations of the interstellar medium in gravity-stratified boxes with the Piernik MHD code. The system contains thermal gas, spectrally resolved cosmic-ray (CR) nuclei within the CRESP algorithm, and spectrally unresolved CR protons. CR protons drive gas outflows and undergo Coulomb and hadronic losses. CR nuclei undergo Coulomb losses. We also include the spallation of primary Carbon by thermal gas atoms, thereby decaying Car- bon into secondary stable Boron isotopes. We use the resistive-MHD approximation as a subgrid model for unresolved turbulent magnetic dissipation in current sheets. We perform a parameter study of the magnetic diffusivity η. The Boron-to-Carbon ratio B/C and hadronic γ-ray luminosity density are computed as diagnostics. We find that non-zero magnetic diffusivity modifies the magnetic topology, leading to a more horizontal field in the halo, thereby accumulating CRs in the disk region and signifi- cantly increasing the CR escape time. As a consequence, the local turbulent conditions and turbulent reconnection in the interstellar medium should be considered when interpreting B/C and hadronic γ-ray luminosity observed at Earth. Our results suggest that the escape time and diffusion coefficient of CRs deduced from observational data in the vicinity of the Solar System cannot be extrapolated to the whole Galaxy.

Comparison of observed and theoretically calculated amplitudes of Forbush effects for events associated with the arrival of a shock wave

P. G. Kobelev, V. N. Zirakashvili, D. N. Dorosheva, V. A. Oleneva, E. G. Klepach, V. G. Yanke

This paper examines the role of plasma compression regions ahead of interplanetary coronal mass ejections in the formation of Forbush effects decreases in cosmic ray intensity during the first phase. The aim of this study is to identify and quantify the relationship between the structure of the CME (specifically, the parameters of the compression region behind the shock front) and the nature of the observed Forbush effect. To this end, events with intensity decrease amplitudes greater than 5 percent were selected, and a theoretical model of cosmic ray behavior at the shock front was tested using modern analytical methods and mathematical models. Analysis of the results for two dozen events revealed satisfactory agreement between the theory and observations. The key reason for some discrepancies is the need to integrate the magnetic field over the particle gyro radius. The obtained data provide insight into the mechanisms of cosmic ray modulation in such environments.

Local Analogs of Little Red Dots: Clues to the X-ray Weakness

Shi-Jiang Chen, Jun-Xian Wang, Zhen-Ya Zheng, Ruqiu Lin, Luis C. Ho, Zhen-Yi Cai, Chang-Hao Chen, Zsofi Igo, Jia-Lai Kang, Ruancun Li, Teng Liu, Wenke Ren, Zhen-Bo Su

A major question about JWST's little red dots (LRDs) at $z\sim4-8$ is the origin of their apparent X-ray weakness. Even deep Chandra stacks yield only tentative detections or broad-band limits and cannot distinguish among super-Eddington accretion, intrinsic X-ray weakness, and Compton-thick (CT) obscuration. Local analogs offer a powerful complementary route. At $z\sim0.3$, similarly luminous sources require $\sim10^{3-4}$ less exposure for comparable signal-to-noise, suffer less source confusion, and directly probe the diagnostic rest-frame soft X-rays. Here we present a pilot Einstein Probe/FXT study of six "local little red dots" (LLRDs), V-shaped broad-line green pea galaxies selected as low-redshift counterparts of LRDs. These sources show X-ray diversity: "X-normal" LLRDs resemble type 1 AGNs in X-ray luminosity and optical variability, whereas the "X-weak" stack lies $\sim0.5-0.7$ dex below standard unobscured-quasar scaling relations while remaining compatible with the representative LRD stack and 95% of published individual limits. The X-weak LLRDs also lack detected optical variability and have redder optical continua than X-normal LLRDs, making their V-shaped SEDs closer to those of high-redshift LRDs. The detection of the X-weak stack at $\sim3.3σ$ with the soft-X-ray-sensitive EP-FXT provides rest-frame $\sim2-3$ keV constraints difficult to reconcile with a typical unobscured super-Eddington model or a local-AGN-like CT geometry examined here, although modified variants remain possible. The simplest alternative is a mildly X-ray-weak corona ($Δα_{\mathrm{ox}}\sim-0.1$ to $-0.2$) with Compton-thin absorption ($N_{\mathrm{H}}\sim10^{22-23}$ cm$^{-2}$), consistent with the independent Balmer decrement/break diagnostics. Together with growing samples of local LRD analogs, our results offer a practical path for testing the physical nature of LRD X-ray weakness.

Estimation Of The Mass And The Spin Of The Neutron Star 4U 1728-34 Using Triples Of X-Ray Quasi-Periodic Oscillations

Radostina P. Tasheva, Ivan Z. Stefanov

X-ray quasi-periodic oscillations (QPOs) were reported for 19 different observations of the atoll source 4U 1728- 34. Three of them reveal triplets of QPOs: a lower and a upper kHz QPOs and a low-frequency QPO. Even though they are discernible only after averaging we assume them to be simultaneous. We apply a separate model (formula) for each of the three frequencies of the observed QPOs and compose a system which contains three algebraic equations for the three unknown parameters - the mass and the specific angular momentum of the central neutron star, and the radius of the orbit of orgin of the given triplet of QPOs, which allows us to find the numerical values of the later. Error propagation formulas are applied for the obtaining of interval estimates of the parameters of the star. This is done through implicit function differentiation. The calculations are repeated with several different models for the kHz QPOs.

A Framework for Analysing Nebular-Phase JWST Supernova Spectra with Application to Type Ic SN 2024aecx

S. Barmentloo, A. Jerkstrand

We analyse what physical quantities of SN ejecta can be obtained from a single epoch of nebular JWST spectroscopy. We provide an analytical framework, which together with a grid of helium star \texttt{SUMO} spectral synthesis models allows the determination of these quantities for any stripped-envelope SN. Using the framework, we derive critical electron densities for major emission lines, finding values in the range $10^5-10^6$ cm$^{-3}$. This means formation under close to LTE conditions for 200 -- 300 days. We obtain ionisation fractions of Ni and Co, as well as the temperature and filling factor of the Fe/He zone, the $^{56}$Ni and stable nickel masses, the Ni/Fe ratio, and the optical depths $τ_{\mathrm{[Ni\,II]\, 6.636 μm}}$ and $τ_{\mathrm{[Co\,II]\, 10.52 μm}}$. Besides these constraints from iron group elements, one can also obtain the filling factor of the carbon burning (O/Ne/Mg) zone from [Ne II] 12.81 $μ$m, and the masses of Ar II and Cl I. We apply our methodology to a publicly available JWST spectrum of Type Ic SN 2024aecx. We find that this SN produced a chlorine mass of $M_{\mathrm{Cl\, I}} \simeq M_{\mathrm{Cl}} = 5.1^{+2.0}_{-1.6}\times 10^{-4}$ $M_{\odot}$, a factor a few larger than expected from stellar evolution modeling. Furthermore, we find that the SN experienced an unexpectedly small 'Ni-bubble' effect, with $ρ_{\mathrm{FeHe}} = 2.5^{+0.7}_{-0.4} \times 10^{-15}$ g cm$^{-3}$ (or an expansion factor of 1 -- 3 for the Ni-bubble, depending on the adopted model) at 167d for the silicon burning material. We also determine $M_{\mathrm{Ar\, II}} = 4.9^{+2.2}_{-1.5} \times 10^{-4}$ $M_{\odot}$, which from ionisation fractions in our NLTE models yields a best estimate of $M_{\mathrm{Ar}} = 1.1 \pm 0.4 \times 10^{-3}$ $M_{\odot}$.

Radio Galaxies as UHECR Sources in Light of Recent Microquasar Results

A. M. Taylor, J. H. Matthews

The recent discovery that microquasars operate as efficient particle accelerators brings back into focus the question as to whether their larger cousins, radio galaxies, also operate as efficient accelerators. We here discuss what we presently know about particle acceleration in radio galaxies, focusing particularly on the nearest such object, Centaurus A.

A Unified Model for Radioactive Signatures and Source Identifiability in Neutron Star Mergers

Hisham Anwer

Late light curves of neutron star mergers can contain substantial fission power while remaining compatible with different radioactive inventories. We develop a unified source attribution model in which a common bounded response formulation links nuclear heating, radiation transport and isotope identification. Within particle dependent deposition and gray photon escape, matched luminosity comparisons show how long alpha and fission responses become aligned. Explicit competing inventories then reproduce a light curve with a substantial $^{254}$Cf contribution, with smooth evolution of the beta luminosity response providing an important source of compensation. We derive optimization certificates and collective likelihood bounds that quantify the resulting limits on identification under specified source and ejecta constraints. We also introduce an energy conserving model with two radiation frequency groups to resolve the information carried by color at fixed total luminosity precision. Its exact response modes identify the source adjustments that absorb color contrast, while a residual color floor characterizes the contrast retained by the permitted family. Late observations of AT~2023vfi with the James Webb and Hubble Space Telescopes provide an observational benchmark: the reference and retained alternatives predict bolometric declines about 1.5 quoted uncertainties shallower than the reconstruction, while differences between inventories containing and excluding $^{254}$Cf remain within the reported decline uncertainty. The unified model provides a quantitative basis for connecting nucleosynthesis predictions, gravitational wave constraints and the temporal and spectral information in merger observations to radioactive source attribution.

Magnetic field effect on neutral color superconductivity as a mechanism to boost the inner magnetic field of magnetars

Bo Feng

In this paper, we review the impact of a strong magnetic field on the neutral two-flavor color superconducting (2SC) phase of dense quark matter. Using a Nambu-Jona-Lasinio (NJL) model approach in the mean-field approximation, we first solve the gap equations for the diquark condensate $Δ$ and the electric chemical potential $μ_e$ under a uniform external magnetic field, and discuss the coupled evolution of the chiral and diquark order parameters. We then evaluate the field-dependent Meissner masses of the rotationally charged gluons for a wide range of densities and moderate diquark coupling strengths. Combining the "zero-mode problem" mechanism with the neutrality condition, we show that the gluon masses become tachyonic even at moderate magnetic-field values, signaling a chromomagnetic instability in density regions where the system was stable at zero field. This instability can be resolved through the restructuring of the ground state with the formation of a gluon vortex condensate. A remarkable consequence of the vortex configuration is the paramagnetic amplification of the in-medium magnetic field: seed fields of the order of $10^{15}$ G can be enhanced by up to two orders of magnitude, reaching $\sim 10^{17}$ G, precisely the range predicted for magnetar cores. We compare this microscopic mechanism with the conventional dynamo explanation and discuss how it offers a plausible explanation for the extremely high magnetic fields expected to be realized in magnetar interiors.

22 Oct 2026

Tilt, warp, and dissipation flare in hydrodynamic disks around kicked compact objects

Yoonsoo Kim

We study the dynamical response of a hydrodynamic disk to an impulsive kick imparted to a central massive object, focusing on the global properties of the material that remains gravitationally bound. We construct a simple analytic model and show that the bound material can acquire a significant tilt---or even a retrograde rotation---if the kick is directed close to the disk midplane. We perform 3D numerical simulations and show that (1) the bound material relaxes to a torus-like circularized flow of which total mass and angular momentum is in fair agreement with the analytic estimate, and (2) in spite of nonlinear hydrodynamic effects, a highly tilted or retrograde disk can be sufficiently formed. The simulations additionally reveal a transient warp in the kicked disk, suggesting a kick as a formation mechanism of tilted or warped disks around compact objects. About 10% of the kinetic energy the bound material acquired from the kick is converted to the internal energy, while the exact conversion efficiency depends on the kick angle and the initial surface mass density profile. The luminosity of this kick-driven dissipation flare can reach $\sim 10^{43}\,{\rm erg\,s^{-1}}$ for strong kicks ($V_{\rm kick} \sim 1000$km/s). We discuss the implications of our results for recoiling supermassive black holes, recoiled stellar-mass black holes in the disks of active galactic nuclei, and natal kicks on compact objects.

The Galactic Center Reference Frame: A 30-Year Solution

Jeremy Darling, Jennie Paine, Matthew Hosek, Shoko Sakai, Stefan Gillessen, Tatsuya Akiba-Lewis, Andrea Ghez, Frank Eisenhauer, Reinhard Genzel

Stellar orbits probe the metric of and mass around the Sgr A* massive black hole. To enable study of the black hole, the surrounding mass, and tests of gravity, a precise reference frame must be established and maintained. We present an updated stellar maser-based reference frame for the inner parsec of the Galactic Center, referenced to the radio continuum of Sgr A*. This work synthesizes 30 years of astrometric observations of 15 SiO stellar masers and the Sgr A* continuum at 43 GHz. The uncertainty in the mean offset of the maser ensemble from Sgr A* is 0.08 mas in the current epoch and will decay to 0.10 mas by 2030. At a distance of 8.2 kpc, these correspond to position uncertainties of 0.6 AU and 0.8 AU, respectively, which is similar to or smaller than the stellar SiO-emitting region. The mean maser proper motion uncertainties are 0.05 mas yr$^{-1}$ in RA and 0.08 mas yr$^{-1}$ in Dec, corresponding to 1.8 km s$^{-1}$ and 3.2 km s$^{-1}$. The reference frame stability between IR and maser astrometry is 10~$μ$as yr$^{-1}$ (0.40 km s$^{-1}$) in RA and 15 $μ$as yr$^{-1}$ (0.57 km s$^{-1}$) in Dec. The masers show substantial variability in flux density and velocity structure, most notably IRS~7, which shows a 30-fold brightening in a blueshifted component since 2022. This variability may set a floor on future astrometry improvements and especially measurements of radial acceleration.

Collapsars Are Not Heavy $r$-Process Factories

Ore Gottlieb, Danat Issa, Jonatan Jacquemin-Ide

Collapsar disks are proposed sources of $r$-process elements, but neutron-rich disk ejecta require gas to be both neutronized in the disk and ejected before accretion. The magnetic field that enables ejection also thickens the disk and accelerates the inflow, lowering both the density and the time available for weak processing. To compute the resulting neutronization, we evolve the electron fraction of the inflowing gas, adopting at each radius the temperature that maximizes the net neutronization rate for the local density and instantaneous composition. This yields a lower limit on $Y_{\rm e}$ that is independent of the disk's thermal history. We adopt an optimistic unbinding radius of $R_{\rm unb}=10\,r_g$ for all magnetic fluxes capable of launching outflows. Combining this ejection condition with favorable assumptions of low black hole mass, ejection of midplane gas, and the upper end of the $γ$-ray burst (GRB) engine-power scale, which limits the accretion rate to $\dot M_{\rm BH}\lesssim0.4\,M_\odot\,{\rm s}^{-1}$, gives a fiducial lower bound $Y_{\rm e}\gtrsim0.33$, above the characteristic $Y_{\rm e}\approx 0.25$ threshold for heavy $r$-process production. Stronger magnetic flux, toward which jet-launching engines evolve, raises the lower bound to $Y_{\rm e}\gtrsim0.49$. We conclude that collapsar disk outflows cannot supply the heavy $r$-process yields invoked for a dominant enrichment channel. Only engines with unusually high accretion rates, potentially including exceptionally luminous GRBs, such as GRB 221009A, and direct-horizon collapsars, may produce heavy $r$-process ejecta.

From Inspiral to Outburst: Modeling a Potential Luminous Red Nova from Betelgeuse

Naunet Leonhardes-Barboza, Andrea Antoni, Morgan MacLeod, Angela Twum, Enrico Ramirez-Ruiz

Recent evidence suggests that Betelgeuse may host a stellar companion whose orbit will decay through tidal interactions, ultimately leading to engulfment and merger within the next several thousand years. If confirmed, this system could represent the nearest and best-characterized progenitor of a future luminous red nova (LRN). We investigate whether the shock-breakout and cooling emission preceding the LRN can be used to constrain the properties of the companion and its disruption within the stellar envelope. Using binary stellar evolution calculations, we evolve Betelgeuse with companions of $0.7$, $1.0$, and $2.0 M_\odot$ to the onset of common envelope evolution (CEE). We then perform one-dimensional radiation-hydrodynamic simulations that follow companion disruption, energy deposition, and shock propagation through the envelope. We find that the peak luminosity increases systematically with companion mass, from $2.44\times10^{41}$ to $6.17\times10^{41}$ erg s$^{-1}$ across the $0.7$-$2.0 M_\odot$ models, while the duration increases more modestly from $1.48$ to $1.99$ days and attains apparent magnitudes brighter than Venus as viewed from Earth. Tidal disruption contributes both kinetic energy and mass to the envelope, linking the companion's structure and mass to the energetics of the shock-heated ejecta. The monotonic luminosity sequence demonstrates that the early breakout emission can retain direct information about the engulfed companion. The interaction ejects $6.15$-$11.19 M_\odot$ of hydrogen-rich material, substantially modifying both the envelope structure and circumstellar environment prior to core collapse.

An Extra-nuclear Water Maser in NGC 1106 Likely Driven By An AGN Shock: Implications for Maser-based Black Hole Mass Measurements

Macon Magno, Emmanuel Momjian, Krista L. Smith, O. Ivy Wong, Travis C. Fischer, Michael J. Koss, Chenoa D. Tremblay

We present Very Large Array A-configuration ($0.13$'') 22 GHz spectral-line and continuum observations of the Swift-BAT detected Seyfert 2 galaxy, NGC 1106, aimed at localizing a water maser line offset from the host systemic velocity by 541 km s$^{-1}$. The 22 GHz continuum emission reveals a 352 pc extended structure within the nucleus of NGC 1106, and we spatially resolve the water maser emission to a compact region $\sim1$ kpc ($3.12$'') from the nucleus. Comparing our data with previous 22 GHz 1'' observations, we found that the water maser lies near the boundary of a radio extension aligned with the jet axis, likely arising from the interface between the active galactic nucleus (AGN) outflow and the host galaxy's ISM. To verify this, we compared the water maser in NGC 1106 to a known water maser in a star-forming region within the Milky Way, along with known extragalactic water masers associated with star formation and AGN driven outflows. Overall, we found that the maser position, velocity offset, flux density, and alignment with the jet axis are consistent with an origin at the interface between the AGN outflow and the host ISM. Our results have important implications for maser-based black hole mass measurements in which disk geometry is ambiguous. Since single-dish observations cannot spatially separate disk masers from outflow-driven masers, high-velocity maser features attributed to the accretion disk may also include contributions from kpc-scale shocks.

Image Morphology of Misaligned Magnetically Arrested Disks

Samuel Feyan, Cora Prather, Dominic O. Chang, Daniel C. M. Palumbo, Koushik Chatterjee, Michael D. Johnson

Horizon scale images probe the plasma dynamics and spacetime in the vicinity of accreting black holes. Interpreting these images requires models that connect image morphology to the structure of the underlying accretion flow. Disk misalignment is expected in many systems, yet most horizon scale image analyses assume that the emitting flow is aligned with the black hole spin. We investigate whether tilted accretion flows produce image features distinguishable from axisymmetric flows and whether their effects can be absorbed by changes in aligned model parameters. Using general relativistic magnetohydrodynamic simulations of tilted magnetically arrested disks spanning black hole spins $a^*=-0.9375$ to $0.9375$ and initial disk tilts $0^\circ$ to $30^\circ$, we generate synthetic images and quantify their morphology through fits to an aligned, semi-analytic emission model. Using this approach, we show that high spin prograde models show little sensitivity to disk tilt, as the inner emitting flow aligns with the black hole spin. In contrast, retrograde tilted disks develop warped, non-axisymmetric inner structure that distorts the inner shadow and strongly alters image morphology. While the aligned semi-analytic model reproduces most prograde images well, retrograde tilted disk images are fit less accurately and yield biased inferred parameters, especially for spin. The image morphologies indicate that tilt affects horizon scale images only when misalignment survives into the near horizon emitting region, motivating image models that include disk tilt as an explicit degree of freedom when alignment is uncertain.

Boosted Dark Matter Directionality in Large Liquid Scintillator Detectors

Samuel S. H. Tse, Qishan Liu, Kenny C. Y. Ng, Koun Choi, Yu-Feng Li

Light dark matter can be boosted by cosmic-ray-dark matter interactions, which opens the possibility of direct-detection searches with large neutrino detectors. We consider the signal produced by boosted dark matter undergoing quasi-elastic scattering off carbon and knocking out a neutron in a large liquid scintillator detector. A distinctive delayed gamma ray from neutron capture can be reconstructed after $\sim 210$ $μ$s, which is paired up with the prompt signal during neutron propagation. We find that 100 kt$\cdot$yr of data from the neutron-coincidence channel will be able to probe new parameter space compared to free proton signals found in previous work. We further consider the possibility of utilizing directional information from the displaced vertex associated with the capture of the final-state neutron. However, we find that directional information does not materially improve the sensitivity due to the large nucleon scattering angle.

Formation and Detectability of PSR J0653+0443-like Extremely Eccentric Binary Neutron Stars in the Milky Way

Zhiwei Chen, Jihui Zhang, Youjun Lu

Recently, the Five-hundred-meter-Aperture-Spherical-radio-Telescope (FAST) Galactic Plane Pulsar Snapshot (GPPS) survey discovers an extraordinary binary neutron star (BNS) system, PSR J0653+0443, possessing the highest known orbital eccentricity ($e\approx0.992$) among Galactic BNSs (Yang et al., 2026), which provides a unique probe into the formation and evolution of BNSs. In this Letter, we perform compact binary population synthesis calculations with self-consistent neutron star spin evolution to model extremely eccentric BNS population detectable by the FAST GPPS survey, explicitly incorporating observational selection effects. We find that BNSs with $e>0.99$ constitute only $\sim0.6\%$ of the detectable population, with an expected detection number of $λ_{\mathrm{GPPS}}\approx0.31$ over the full planned footprint. Thus, although rare, systems as eccentric as PSR J0653+0443 are compatible with standard binary evolution. We further demonstrate that extremely eccentric BNSs arise preferentially from core-collapse supernovae, whereas imposing $P\lesssim0.1$ s select older, more frequently recycled systems, which naturally reproduce the observed properties of PSR J0653+0443. We further predict that the completed GPPS sample has a $\sim15\%$ chance of finding another $e>0.99$ BNS, but a much higher $\sim88\%$ chance for $e>0.90$ systems. By contrast, future facilities are expected to enlarge the detectable highly eccentric BNS population, with SKA potentially detecting PSR J0653+0443-like systems and sub-mHz gravitational-wave detectors probing a larger population with $e>0.9$, enabling much more in-depth population-level constraints.

Charged fluid tori with an approximate magnetic self-field around magnetized Schwarzschild black holes: A semi-analytic approach

Abylaikhan Tlemissov, Jiri Kovar

We study toroidal structures formed by a charged, non-self-gravitating perfect fluid orbiting a Schwarzschild black hole immersed in a poloidal magnetic field. To obtain analytic and semi-analytic solutions, we assume a cold, rest-mass-dominated fluid with uniform specific charge and zero conductivity, so the current is purely convective. This regime is formally opposite to the ideal GRMHD limit. The total magnetic field comprises a prescribed asymptotically uniform external component and an approximate self-field associated with the azimuthal current in the torus. We represent the latter by a regularized dipole approximation to the field of an effective circular current loop. Neglecting the self-field, the method of characteristics yields a general implicit solution of the integrability condition for a function parametrizing the specific-angular-momentum profile. A separately obtained cylindrically dependent class recovers a solution from our earlier work. For the radially dependent class, we integrate the Euler equations, obtain the enthalpy potential, and derive local conditions for equatorially centered tori. We then examine the field-free limit, both magnetic components separately, and their superposition. The external field supports closed tori within analytically derived parameter bounds, whereas no such equilibrium is found for the isolated approximate self-field within the numerically explored domain. In a representative combined-field sequence, the radial extent varies nonmonotonically with relative self-field strength: it increases for weak self-fields and decreases as the self-field becomes comparable to the external field. The results show how an approximate magnetic self-field modifies the morphology and domain of existence of charged-fluid tori.

A Generalized Framework for Rapid Polarization Response Generation in Wide Field-of-View Compton Polarimeters

Mohamed Gamal Zaid, Merlin Kole, Mark McConnell, Fabio Muleri, Hancheng Li

Polarimetry is the most direct probe of the unsettled radiation mechanism and magnetic field structure of gamma-ray burst (GRB) prompt emission at the energies where it peaks, tens of keV to about 1 MeV. Compton polarimeters use the azimuthal scattering angle distribution (ASAD), a 180 degree modulation on axis. GRB measurements have largely relied on the on-axis modulation factor $μ_{100}$. For a wide field-of-view planar polarimeter, GRBs arrive off axis, where the Klein-Nishina projection adds harmonics to the ASAD. Muleri (2014) described this projection and evaluated it in a low-energy expansion for X-rays. We evaluate it numerically without approximation over the whole prompt-emission energy band for any incidence and polarization angles, quantify the off-axis harmonics a single modulation factor cannot capture, and verify the model against GEANT4 simulations of an ideal polarimeter. We also include the effect of the detection threshold on the ASAD. With POLAR as the case study, we present two modes that build the full polarization response with at least 95 percent fewer Monte Carlo simulations. Mode 1 takes only the unpolarized simulation per energy and recovers the polarization measured with POLAR's full GEANT4 responses for all but the brightest GRBs, where it underestimates the polarization degree. Mode 2 adds two polarized simulations per energy at distinct angles and reproduces the simulated ASADs to their statistical noise and the polarization of every GRB tested. Both modes are released as open-source software for any planar Compton polarimeter.

Prompt Jet Launching and Delayed Thermal Disk in Tidal Disruption Event for Prolonged Activity GRB 220706A

Yuri Sato, Rin Oikawa

GRB 220706A exhibits X-ray flaring activity extending to ~51 days after the trigger, along with a luminous optical thermal component emerging on a timescale of ~10^6 s. Such prolonged central-engine activity could be powered by a collapsar fed by an extended stellar envelope. However, a relativistic jet may struggle to break out of the extended envelope. These challenges motivate a tidal disruption event (TDE), although explaining both the early relativistic jet and the luminous late optical thermal emission within one TDE framework remains difficult. We investigate a TDE scenario in which shocks between stellar debris streams drive the early inflow of some debris, potentially carrying preexisting fossil magnetic flux toward the black hole (BH) and powering an initial relativistic jet via the Blandford-Znajek mechanism. The remaining debris circularizes into an accretion disk, whose thermal emission accounts for the late optical thermal component. For GRB 220706A, the optical timescale, luminosity, and color temperature are consistent with disruption of a blue supergiant (M_*~50M_{sun}, R_*~20~R_{sun}) by a BH of mass M_{BH}~10^5 M_{sun}, assuming that the accretion disk forms on a timescale comparable to the orbital period of the most tightly bound stellar debris. A magnetic field strength of ~10^5 G is required near the BH before disruption to power a jet capable of producing the inferred gamma-ray luminosity. Continued fallback may sustain central-engine activity beyond the observed X-ray flaring episode, making future X-ray monitoring a test of this scenario.

Simulation-based inference of profile evolution for high-precision pulsar timing

Sai C. Susarla, Gregory Ashton, A. Parthasarathy, K. De Man, J. P. W. Verbiest

Pulsars are exceptional cosmic laboratories, the most stable of which rival atomic clocks. Nearly all of that precision rests on one observable, the average pulse profile unique to each pulsar and stable over decades. These profiles change shape systematically with observing frequency, reflecting the physics of the emitting pulsar beam, but if left unmodelled, it can bias the arrival-time measurements, undermining precision timing. Profile-domain timing has been proposed to address this effect, but current implementations use stochastic sampling, which is computationally prohibitive and has limited the application in large-scale analyses. Here, we present a simulation-based inference approach to profile-domain timing, using a normalising flow conditioned on a convolutional embedding of the frequency-phase portrait. From a single portrait, it returns joint posteriors on dispersion measures, arrival times, and the parameters governing profile evolution instantaneously, with uncertainties verified against known inputs following an initial training run. The value of a likelihood-free approach is that the simulator becomes the interface to pulsar properties. Fundamental properties like non-Gaussian noise, pulsar self-noise, jitter, scattering, and profile change can be inferred by simulating them rather than by deriving a new likelihood for each, opening a promising pathway to include machine learning methods into pulsar timing workflows.

Newtonian I-Love-Q relations: An enthalpy-based approach

Athul Kunjipurayil, J. Piekarewicz

We present a pedagogical introduction to the I-Love-Q relations within Newtonian gravity using an enthalpy-based formulation of stellar structure. Introducing the specific enthalpy converts Euler's equation into a first integral of hydrostatic equilibrium and recasts the equation of state as a relation between the mass density and the enthalpy. Together with Poisson's equation, these relations provide a unified description of spherical equilibrium and deformations induced by rotation and tides. We illustrate the formalism with polytropic equations of state and a relativistic free Fermi gas. In Newtonian gravity, slow uniform rotation and a weak tidal field excite the same linear quadrupolar response, yielding the exact identity $\bar I^{\,2}\bar Q/\barλ\equiv1$ for the dimensionless (``bar'') observables---independently of the equation of state. This exact identity is distinguished from the approximate universality of the pairwise I-Love-Q relations, whose residual dependence on stellar structure is illustrated by the models considered in this work.

Gravitational Wave Diffractive Images Reveal the Structure of Astrophysical Objects

Han Gil Choi, Sunghoon Jung, Volodymyr Takhistov

Scattering patterns encode the structure of extended objects, as demonstrated by charged particle scattering on atomic nuclei. Gravitational wave (GW) diffraction offers an analogous probe of otherwise invisible mass distributions. At linear order in the lens potential we derive expressions relating the central diffractive image contribution to a gravitationally lensed GW waveform to the Fourier transform of the projected lens density. For finite mass lenses this contribution takes the form of a point mass Born amplitude multiplied by a normalized mass form factor, analogous to that in nuclear scattering. As the frequency of GWs from a merging compact binary source evolves the signal samples this form factor across spatial scales, imprinting lens structure through interference with the primary image. Using full numerical wave optics calculations we quantify the observational reach for extended dark matter objects, primordial black holes halo-dressed by dark matter halos and superradiance clouds of ultralight bosons around black holes. For the considered source populations, five year observations with next generation detectors can distinguish extended lenses from point masses, probing dark matter fractions down to $\mathcal{O}(10^{-5})$, and halo-dressed primordial black holes from matched bare black holes, probing primordial black hole dark matter fractions down to $\mathcal{O}(10^{-6})$. For sufficiently close source-lens configurations, scalar superradiance clouds can produce distinguishable diffraction signatures for boson masses of approximately $10^{-17}-10^{-12}~\mathrm{eV}$. These results provide a framework for characterizing extended dark matter and compact object astrophysical environments.

Synthetic 2D magnetic turbulence with controlled island structuring

Yari De Paep, Daniela Maci, Rony Keppens

Magnetic turbulence remains up to this day not fully understood, despite its important role in the behavior of plasmas. A recurring ingredient in plasma turbulence is the spontaneous formation and evolution of plasmoids. These represent magnetic islands on current sheets in two-dimensional turbulent magnetic fields that are consistently found in high-resolution direct numerical simulations (DNSs) using a magnetohydrodynamic description. However, such simulations require considerable amounts of computational power. Synthetic turbulence models provide an alternative, cheap approach to the generation of fully-developed turbulent fields. In this work, we first present an extension of our open-source, general synthetic turbulence BxC toolkit that is specifically tailored to generate 2D turbulent magnetic fields. We then use a novel physically motivated, but purely geometric construction that generates current sheet island chains, which results in fields that visually match the ones obtained through DNSs. We show how this construction affects the magnetic field lines and find a correlation with the formation of magnetic O-points and X-points. The magnetic flux and size of the islands in the field are found to match well with theoretical scaling laws. Lastly, we show BxC's capabilities by generating a 2D $N\times N$ turbulent magnetic field of resolution $N = 100000$, going beyond the state-of-the-art reached by simulations, on a single CPU with modest computational resources. Our construction maintains direct control over the inertial range, and allows to make morphological changes to the plasmoids and fields, demonstrating plasmoid appearance, disappearance and merging.

FRB 20210601A: Extreme Temporal Clustering in a Repeating Fast Radio Burst Source as Evidence for a Hidden Class of Episodic Repeaters

Ryan Mckinven, Amanda M. Cook, Alice P. Curtin, B. M. Gaensler, Jason W. T. Hessels, Naman Jain, Lordrick Kahinga, Victoria M. Kaspi, Charanjot Brar, Tomas Cassanelli, Shami Chatterjee, Fengqiu Adam Dong, Maxwell Fine, Emmanuel Fonseca, Afrokk Khan, Bikash Kharel, Calvin Leung, Robert Main, Kiyoshi W. Masui, Kyle McGregor, Nicole Mulyk, Mason Ng, Kenzie Nimmo, Ayush Pandhi, Aaron B. Pearlman, Ziggy Pleunis, Mawson W. Sammons, Paul Scholz, Kaitlyn Shin, Seth R. Siegel, Kendrick Smith, Ingrid Stairs

Population studies of fast radio burst (FRB) repetition typically model sources using a single distribution of steady, time-averaged burst rates, yet individual sources show significant departures from this assumption. Here we present FRB 20210601A, a source discovered by the Canadian Hydrogen Intensity Mapping Experiment FRB project (CHIME/FRB) that represents the most extreme such departure yet identified. The source produced at least nine bursts within a single 106.5 s interval -- the highest instantaneous burst rate yet reported from a repeating FRB source at $\gtrsim 2$ Jy ms fluences -- yet remained otherwise undetected despite $\sim 320$ hr of exposure spanning 7 yr. The active interval occurred while the source was in a side-lobe of the CHIME/FRB digitized beams, implying an instantaneous burst rate $\gtrsim2.3\times$ higher ($\gtrsim$ 21 bursts) at main-lobe sensitivity. The source's intense, brief burst storm flanked by apparent quiescence despite substantial exposure is unique among the CHIME/FRB repeater sample, although its burst morphology, excess dispersion measure ($\rm{DM_{excess}} \sim 360$ pc cm$^{-3}$), Faraday rotation measure ($|\rm{RM_{excess}}| \sim 150-170$ rad m$^{-2}$), and temporal scattering ($τ\lesssim 0.37$ ms at 600 MHz) are otherwise typical. Modeling a population of episodically active repeaters (i.e., short burst storms separated by long recurrence times) and accounting for declination-dependent exposure, we forecast $\sim1-4$ additional source discoveries similar to FRB 20210601A over the next decade of CHIME/FRB operations, corresponding to a CHIME/FRB-detectable population of $9^{+200}_{-8}$ sources (68% credible interval). These results demonstrate that highly episodic repeaters could constitute a selection-biased but detectable component of the FRB population, possibly reflecting distinct magnetospheric states or triggering mechanisms.

Spectral Properties of X-Ray Flares in Gamma-Ray Bursts

Xiongwei Bi, Zejun Jiang, Jirong Mao

We conducted a systematic spectral analysis of the X-ray flares of gamma-ray bursts (GRBs) observed by Swift/X-ray Telescope from 2005 April to 2015 March. The sample includes 521 flares from 206 GRBs. The GRBs cover the three subtypes: X-ray flashes (XRFs), X-ray-rich GRBs (XRRs), and classical GRBs (C-GRBs). We used a simple power-law model to fit the spectra during the flares, as well as to fit the preflare and postflare afterglow emissions, and obtained the parameters such as photon index and intrinsic hydrogen column density ($N_{\rm H}$). By comparing the spectral characteristics of flares, prompt emission, and afterglow emission, and analyzing the temporal evolution of the X-ray flare spectra, we obtained the following main conclusions. (1) The spectrum during the flare phase is significantly harder than that of the preflare and postflare phases, while there is no obvious difference in the spectrum between the preflare and postflare phases. (2) The intrinsic $N_{\rm H}$ shows no significant change between the preflare and flare phases, but significantly decreases after the flare. (3) The overall X-ray flare spectra are softer than those of the prompt emission. (4) For GRBs with multiple flares, the photon index softens with increasing flare peak time. (5) The X-ray flare spectra in XRFs are softer than those in C-GRBs. These results support the origin of X-ray flares being from the internal shock radiation driven by the late activity of the GRB central engine.

Multigroup Radiation Diffusion with Compton Scattering on a Moving Mesh: Implementation in RICH and Application to Tidal Disruption Events

Itamar Giron, Menahem Krief, Elad Steinberg

Compton scattering exchanges energy between radiation and hot gas and changes the spectrum in high-energy astrophysical systems such as tidal disruption events (TDEs). Because it couples all photon energies and can act faster than the hydrodynamic time step, many radiation-hydrodynamics (RHD) simulations omit its spectral redistribution or treat its energy exchange in the gray approximation. We extend the multigroup flux-limited diffusion module of the moving-mesh RHD code RICH to include Compton scattering with the exact thermal Compton kernel. In our semi-implicit scheme for the thermal radiative transfer equation, the temperature dependence of the kernel enters a generalized Fleck factor, and the group Compton operator conserves photon number and satisfies detailed balance. In equilibration tests, the scheme agrees with independent solutions, relaxes without absorption and emission to a Bose-Einstein spectrum, and remains stable for time steps that do not resolve the Compton equilibration timescale. We apply it to a three-dimensional simulation of the disruption of a $0.5\,M_\odot$ star by a $10^6\,M_\odot$ black hole. We find that during the X-ray flash, inverse Compton scattering dominates the coupling between the gas and the radiation near the nozzle shock, where it adds X-ray energy much faster than thermal emission and may affect the dynamics. Outside the X-ray thermalization radii, its instantaneous X-ray production rate during the flash is comparable to or larger than the escaping X-ray luminosity, so it may contribute substantially to the X-ray emission from TDEs.

Breaking the symmetry: X-ray polarization from a spreading layer on a tilted weakly magnetized neutron star

Anna Bobrikova, Alexandra Veledina, Juri Poutanen, Vladislav Loktev

When modeling the emission from an X-ray binary system, we often assume that the rotational axis of the compact object is aligned with that of the binary system. While often considered irrelevant for spectroscopic observations, the orientation of the binary system is crucial for polarimetric properties. With the recent advent of X-ray polarimetric observations enabled by the Imaging X-ray Polarimetry Explorer (IXPE), indications of a possible misalignment of the neutron star rotational axis with respect to the binary axis have been reported. Following these indications, we present an analytical model for the polarimetric properties of emission arising from a spreading layer on an inclined weakly magnetized neutron star (WMNS). We discuss the expected polarimetric properties and study the impact of geometrical parameters of the system on the polarization of WMNS emission. We compare our results with those for aligned systems. Finally, we discuss applications of the model to recent and upcoming X-ray polarimetric observations with IXPE and mention the suggestions for further development of the model.

In Search of the Gravitational Wave of Fast X-Ray Transient in the Second Part of the LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA Fourth Observing Run

Po-Ya Wang, Albert K. H. Kong, Surojit Saha

Fast X-ray Transients (FXTs) are brief, intense X-ray flashes unassociated with persistent X-ray sources or known stellar objects, typically lasting from seconds to hours. With the launch of the Einstein Probe in January 2024, the study of FXTs has entered a new phase. Its large field of view significantly increases the discovery rate, enabling more robust statistics. The leading discoveries have revealed a fraction of gamma-ray bursts, while the rest remain unexplained. We propose a methodology to search for possible relations between gravitational waves (GWs) and FXTs, as it may serve as a discrepancy among proposed theoretical models. To achieve this, we intend to utilize X-Pipeline, an unmodeled search algorithm, to identify excess coherent signals in LIGO-Virgo-KAGRA (LVK) O4b observations. Out of 64 FXTs events, we obtained 19 FXTs for constructing robust searches. Given the absence of any detectable GW signals, we subsequently calculated upper limits and estimated exclusion distances for each source. In order to enrich the sample base and statistical analysis, this methodology could henceforth be conducted through future GW searches, including KAGRA in the O4c and O5 observations.

The XMM-Newton view of the DEM L36 superbubble in the Large Magellanic Cloud

Pawel M. Janas, Patrick J. Kavanagh, Manami Sasaki, Miroslav D. Filipović, Chandreyee Maitra, Frank Haberl, Pierre Maggi, Sean Points, Zachary J. Smeaton

We present the first multi-wavelength analysis of the DEM L36 superbubble in the Large Magellanic Cloud. We compare the DEM L36 superbubble with other Galactic and Magellanic Cloud superbubbles. We use data from XMM-Newton, supplemented by data from the Magellanic Cloud Emission Line Survey, the Dark Energy Camera Magellanic Cloud Emission Line Survey, the Spitzer Space Telescope, and the Australia Telescope Compact Array. We perform a multi-wavelength morphological analysis to understand the shape and structure of the superbubble and X-ray spectral analysis to derive physical parameters of the hot gas. We find significant X-ray emission in DEM L36, dominated by a central bubble region mostly confined by a thin H$α$ filamentary shell. This has two distinct blowout regions to the east and west, with a possible smaller blowout in the south. Spectral analysis shows the X-ray emission is well fit with a thermal plasma model in collisional ionisation equilibrium with a temperature of $kT=0.17~(0.16-0.18)$ keV and unabsorbed luminosity $L_{X~(0.5-7.0~\mathrm{keV})}=3.62\times 10^{35} $erg s$^{-1}$. Unlike other LMC superbubbles, we find no clear powering cluster at the centre, though relatively small clusters near the edge are likely supplying the mechanical input at the present epoch. We use photometric data of these clusters to estimate cluster masses, ages, and mechanical inputs to compare with the energy stored in the superbubble. We find that these clusters have supplied more energy over their lifetime than is currently observed in the superbubble, indicative of the so-called growth rate discrepancy problem. It is likely the DEM L36 superbubble was initially shaped by a central massive stellar population that has since dispersed and largely lost to supernovae, with smaller clusters at the edges of the swept-up shell being currently supplying the energy input.

Parameter Constraints on $f(R^{1+ε},T_{μν}T^{μν})$ Gravity from Exotic Compact Objects

S. Wilhelmina, B. N. Jayawiguna, A. Sulaksono

According to General Relativity (GR), gravity is described as the curvature of spacetime caused by mass and energy. Although GR has been highly successful in describing gravitational phenomena, exotic compact objects with high mass and strong gravitational fields, as well as low-mass objects, have motivated the study of modified theories of gravity. One of the modified gravity frameworks is $f(R,T^2) = R^{1 + ε} + ακ^2 T_{μν} T^{μν}$, which provides an extension of GR by introducing modifications to both the geometric and matter sectors. In this study, we investigate the mass-radius structure, physical viability, and stability of quark stars in $f(R,T^2)$ gravity using the MIT Bag Model equation of state. We compare results from the exact $f(R,T^2)$ model, its first-order Taylor expansion, and GR. The values of the gravity parameter $ε$ and $α$ are constrained by evaluating the ability of the model to reproduce the observed mass-radius configurations of compact objects, including HESS J1731-347 with a mass of $M = 0.77_{-0.17}^{+0.20} \, M_{\odot}$ and a radius of $R = 10.4_{-0.78}^{+0.86}$ km, GW190814 with a mass of $M = 2.6 \, M_{\odot}$, PSR J1614-2230, with a mass of $M = 1.928 \pm 0.017 \, M_{\odot}$ and a radius of $R > 10$ km, and PSR J0437-4715, with a mass of $M = 1.418 \pm 0.037 \, M_{\odot}$ and a radius of $R = 11.36_{-0.63}^{+0.95}$ km. We assess the physical viability and stability of the stellar configurations using the energy conditions (strong, weak, null, and dominant) and the speed of sound. Overall, the $f(R,T^2)$ gravity model can accommodate quark star configurations and reproduce the observed properties of massive compact objects, particularly those associated with GW190814 and the lightest compact object observed, HESS J1731-347, respectively.

Weak Radio and Bright Early Optical Emission in EP260321a: A Comparative Study with XRO 080109 Reveals the Role of Circumstellar Material Mass

Cui-Yuan Dai, Xiang-Yu Wang, Jian-He Zheng

Shock breakout (SBO) occurs when a radiation-mediated shock wave, formed during a core-collapse supernova (SN) explosion, breaks out from the stellar surface of the progenitor or from the circumstellar material (CSM), if the CSM is optically thick. The X-ray outburst XRO 080109, associated with a Type Ib/c SN 2008D, has been suggested to be an SBO event from a regular supernova. The recent detection of EP260321a, associated with a broad-lined Type Ic SN 2026gzf, by the Einstein Probe adds a new SBO event. However, the two SBO events differ markedly in their spectra, energetics, late-time radio emission and early optical emission within the first day. We show that the non-detections in radio follow-up observations of EP260321a imply a shock velocity $\lesssim0.1c$, whereas the radio data of XRO 080109 suggest a shock velocity of $\simeq0.2c$. These velocities are consistent with those inferred from the properties of the X-ray outbursts and explain the difference in their X-ray spectra. The X-ray energy and duration of EP260321a indicate that the shock breaks out from the CSM at a radius of $\sim2\times10^{13}\ {\rm cm}$. Its bright optical emission at $t\lesssim1\ {\rm d}$ can be explained by cooling emission from a dense, extended CSM shell heated by the radiation-mediated shock, requiring a total CSM mass of $\sim0.06M_\odot$, substantially larger than that inferred for XRO 080109. Finally, the lower shock velocity and larger CSM mass can be unified in a picture in which a more massive CSM decelerates the radiation-mediated shock more significantly before it breaks out.

Zhang, X. Zhang, X. L. Zhang, X. P. Zhang, Yi Zhang, Yong Zhang, Z. P. Zhang, Z. H. Zhang, J. Zhao, L. Zhao, L. Z. Zhao, S. P. Zhao, X. H. Zhao, F. Zheng, T. C. Zheng, B. Zhou, H. Zhou, J. N. Ultra-relativistic magnetic monopoles traversing the atmosphere are expected to initiate extensive air showers characterized by a muon deficit and a compact lateral profile. Using 1438.81 live days of data from the LHAASO-KM2A array, we present a search for these cosmic particles within the Lorentz factor range of $10^5$ to $10^7$. A machine-learning classifier utilizing lateral shower shape observables from the electromagnetic detectors is deployed to separate the signal from the hadronic background. No statistical excess above the background expectation is observed, and upper limits at the 90\% confidence level are established on the isotropic monopole flux. The resulting upper limit is $2.6 \times 10^{-16}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,\mathrm{sr}^{-1}$ at $γ= 10^5$ and reaches $8.8 \times 10^{-19}\,\mathrm{cm}^{-2}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}\,\mathrm{sr}^{-1}$ at $γ= 10^7$, which improves upon the astrophysical Parker bound by over three orders of magnitude. This work establishes constraints on the monopole flux within a previously unconstrained velocity window.

EP J062132.0+623837: A Multi-wavelength Study of a Changing-look Active Galactic Nucleus Discovered by Einstein Probe

Lin-Na Xu, Ning Jiang, Tinggui Wang, Shifeng Huang, Yibo Wang, Zhenfeng Sheng, Mingxin Wu, Jiazheng Zhu, Huaqing Cheng, Chichuan Jin, Jun-Jie Jin, Dongyue Li, Yuan Liu, Jianwei Lyu, Jian-Guo Wang, Yun-Fei Xu, Yongquan Xue, Zhen Zhang

EP J062132.0+623837 is a new X-ray source identified in 14-day binned survey data from the Wide-field X-ray Telescope aboard the Einstein Probe (EP). Subsequent X-ray observations with the Follow-up X-ray Telescope (FXT) on board EP localized the source to the central region of the galaxy 2MASX J06213207+6238390 at $z=0.084$. Here, we present a comprehensive analysis of the multi-wavelength data for this object. During the FXT and Swift/XRT follow-up, the source remained X-ray active, with observed luminosities of $(3-9)\times10^{43}$ erg s$^{-1}$, and its X-ray spectra were well described by power-law models with photon indices of $Γ\sim1.5$. The optical spectrum of the host galaxy clearly reveals a broad-line active galactic nucleus (AGN) exhibiting significantly enhanced broad Balmer emission lines compared to an archival spectrum obtained four years earlier, thereby marking a changing-look transition from Type 1.8 to Type 1.5. Furthermore, long-term optical and mid-infrared light curves indicate that the nucleus underwent dramatic brightening around 2010 and has since shown substantial variability. Given the spatial coincidence and multi-wavelength behavior, we identify EP J062132.0+623837 as a changing-look AGN, initially detected by EP and subsequently confirmed to exhibit large-amplitude variability across multiple bands. This case study demonstrates the great potential of wide-field time-domain X-ray surveys for discovering changing-look AGNs, paving a new avenue for their systematic identification as a powerful complement to ongoing optical surveys.

Relativistic Dynamical Evolution of Kilonova Spectral Emission

Sara Karimi, Soroush Shakeri

We develop a relativistic dynamical model for the thermal evolution of expanding kilonova ejecta, in which the effective temperature is determined by the ejecta mass, opacity, velocity, and radioactive heating rate. The resulting thermal evolution follows a broken power law as the ejecta transition from the optically thick to the optically thin regime. We incorporate this thermal history into a relativistic spectral calculation that accounts for the equal-arrival-time surface geometry and Doppler boosting, enabling us to model the observed kilonova spectrum and constrain the underlying ejecta properties. We investigate the effects of the model parameters and relativistic corrections on the observed temperature and spectral shape, and find that light-travel-time delays and Doppler shifts can produce significant spectral distortions at high ejecta velocities. We apply the model to the early-time X-shooter spectrum of the kilonova AT2017gfo and infer the ejecta mass, opacity, and velocity through Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) fitting with a Student's $t$ likelihood. We find that relativistic effects become increasingly important for fast ejecta ($v \gtrsim 0.5c$), particularly at large viewing angles, where the observed temperature can differ substantially from the rest-frame effective temperature. These results highlight the importance of incorporating relativistic and equal-arrival-time effects when interpreting early-time kilonova spectra, especially for rapidly expanding ejecta.

Lomb-Scargle Recovery of Periodic Binary Signals across Black Hole Mass and Cadence

Karan Akbari, Sayantan Bhattacharya

Searches for periodic supermassive black hole binaries in quasar light curves rely on recovery fractions, the share of injected signals detected, which forecasts use as detection efficiencies. Lin, Charisi, & Haiman (2026) measured them for the Lomb-Scargle periodogram on damped-random-walk light curves matched to 12,400 bright, high-mass Palomar Transient Factory (PTF) quasars with Sloan Digital Sky Survey Data Release 7 (DR7) masses. To test them over four decades of mass, we inject their sinusoidal and sawtooth signals into light curves of mergers in the TNG100-1 cosmological simulation, with magnitudes drawn from DR7 quasars of similar mass (extrapolated at low mass). We apply their threshold in each frequency bin, set by $10^5$ noise-only light curves per binary, on observed PTF cadences, one Stripe 82 cadence and an LSST-like cadence (Legacy Survey of Space and Time). At fixed absolute magnitude $M_i=-23$, sinusoid recovery falls toward high mass, and with DR7 magnitudes it climbs above about $10^8\,M_\odot$, so the magnitude assignment sets the sign of the trend. Above $10^8\,M_\odot$, where their sample lies, PTF sinusoid recovery is 39.8 against their 44.7 per cent. At LSST-like cadence sinusoid recovery is 76.9 per cent, about three times their 22.6 per cent, for reasons mostly unidentified. Denser sampling of the same PTF baselines did not lower recovery in any test. Even at LSST-like cadence we miss most sawtooth signals above $10^8\,M_\odot$. We tabulate recovery per 0.5-dex mass bin and cadence, with low-mass values likely too high for a deeper survey.

Axial Perturbations of Extended Tolman III in the New $f(R,T)$ Theory

B. N. Jayawiguna, G. A. Sabian, H. S. Ramadhan, A. Sulaksono

We investigate compact objects using the extended Tolman III equation of state in the framework of $f(R,T)=R+2χT$ gravity. In the matter sector of the Lagrangian density, we employ a new formalism based on the evaluation of the second-order derivative of the matter Lagrangian with respect to the metric \cite{Jayawiguna:2026das}. We analyze the corresponding observational signatures through axial gravitational perturbations including stellar stability and gravitational echo. We find that the effective potential in $f(R,T)$ gravity is wider and deeper than its counterpart in general relativity (GR). The quasinormal-mode (QNM) frequencies are calculated using the Bohr--Sommerfeld (BS) fitting and both the lower- and higher-order Wentzel--Kramers--Brillouin (WKB) approximations. For the ultracompact configurations considered, trapped modes can tunnel through the potential barrier, giving rise to gravitational-wave echoes. The imaginary parts of the QNM frequencies are found to be negative, $\Im(ω_n M)<0$, indicating that the perturbations decay exponentially with time. Our results show that the echo train in GR contains more echoes than that in $f(R,T)$ gravity. Finally, assuming that the stellar object is a neutron star and adopting a stellar radius consistent with measurements by the Neutron Star Interior Composition Explorer (NICER), we find that the $f(R,T)$ gravity model yields a characteristic frequency consistent with the estimated frequency associated with GW170817 for $β= 4.663\times10^{-1}$ and $χ=0.96π$, whereas the corresponding GR model does not exhibit comparable agreement.

Search for high-energy neutrinos from Einstein Probe detected Fast X-ray Transients using 14 years of IceCube muon track data

Sri Devaki Meduri, Shantanu Desai

We perform a time-integrated search for high energy neutrinos from 27 fast X-ray transients (FXTs) detected by Einstein Probe, using 14 years of publicly available IceCube muon-track data. We employ the unbinned maximum likelihood method which is used for IceCube point source searches. We do not find any statistically significant excess from any of the FXTs. The largest TS value is obtained for EP240801a (TS=4.77), corresponding to a local $p$-value of 0.04, which is not significant. We therefore calculate 90\% confidence level upper limit on the differential muon neutrino flux. The most stringent upper limit is obtained for EP241021a, with $E^2 Φ(E)=2.1\times 10^{-10}$ (GeV cm$^{-2}$ s$^{-1}$), for a $E^{-2}$ spectrum at a pivot energy of 100 TeV.

Early panchromatic spectra reveal helium in the thermonuclear supernova 2024kce

Abigail Polin, Dillon Hasenour, Sahana Kumar, Willem Hoogendam, Jing Lu, Daniel Polin, Danielle Dickinson, Braden Garretson, Nidia Morrell, Huei Sears, Katie Auchettl, Teresa Boland, Christopher Burns, Kenneth C. Chambers, Dhvanil Desai, Jason T. Hinkle, Thressay Hoover, Eric Hsiao, Mark E. Huber, David O. Jones, Colin Macrie, Bailey Martin, Cameron Pfeffer, Miranda Pikus, Benjamin J. Shappee, Madeline Taylor, Brad E. Tucker, Michael Tucker, Huangfei Xiao

Faint Type I supernovae blur the boundaries between spectroscopic classes, obscuring their progenitors and explosion mechanisms. One proposed origin is a double detonation, in which ignition of an accreted helium shell triggers the explosion of a carbon oxygen white dwarf. Surviving helium is a direct prediction of this mechanism, but its strongest near-infrared feature overlaps with carbon and is ambiguous in isolation. Here we present SN 2024kce, observed with simultaneous optical and near-infrared spectroscopy eleven days before maximum light. We identify He I absorption at three wavelengths, all consistent with a velocity of 16,400 km/s, well above the photosphere. This is the first detection of helium before maximum light in a thermonuclear supernova. Helium at these velocities requires nearby radioactive material for non-thermal excitation, pointing to ash from a detonated surface helium shell. A double detonation model reproduces the optical spectral evolution, suppressed blue flux, and He I 1.083 micron absorption feature. SN 2024kce shares properties with both subluminous Type Ia supernovae and Ia-like calcium-rich transients, providing evidence that the same explosion mechanism operates across these classes. From early optical and near-infrared spectra to Rubin's First Look images, SN 2024kce showcases the full reach of modern observatories in exposing the physics of rare explosions.

Photometric classification and out-of-taxonomy detection of ZTF transients with FLARE

Argyro Sasli, Felipe Fontinele Nunes, Xuanrui Zhang, Dimitrios Kourtesis, Saina Nikmehr, Alexandra Junell, Jesper Sollerman, Antoine Le Calloch, Sushant Sharma Chaudhary, Sneha Maharjan, Maojie Xu, Avyukt Raghuvanshi, Steven L. Groom, Joahan Castaneda Jaimes, Josiah Purdum, Roger Smith, Mansi M. Kasliwal, Matthew J. Graham, Ashish A. Mahabal, Michael Coughlin

Photometric classifiers decide which of the million nightly alerts of time-domain surveys receive one of the few thousand spectra taken each year, and they are the first place where a transient outside the trained classes can be noticed. We present flare, the photometric classification and anomaly-detection module of AppleCiDEr, evaluated on 10 497 spectroscopically confirmed ZTF transients in six classes and 53 objects from families outside the taxonomy. Catalogue context raises the macro F1 score, with the largest gains where the defining information is absent from the light curve. Blind detection recovers few out-of-taxonomy objects at a 1% false-alarm rate, while a simulated review loop recovers 83% of them after inspecting 15% of the stream. The first confirmations of a new family are most useful in the feature space: with the same eleven labels, a head on the frozen features detects 68% of the tidal disruption events hidden from the training against 57% for a combiner over the anomaly scores, and retraining with them as a new class reaches 85%. The module runs in production on Fritz, the ZTF instance of SkyPortal, and its alarm is reported as a false-alarm rate per unit time on the stream, calibrated on the photometry that the service receives

Multiwavelength Classification of Chandra Sources in Omega Centauri

Steven Chen, Oleg Kargaltsev, Jeremy Hare, Hui Yang, Oleg Smirnov, Ian Heywood, Talon Myburgh

We present a comprehensive study of X-ray sources in the globular cluster NGC 5139 to date. Over 200 Chandra sources are cross-matched to multiwavelength catalogs from HST, JWST, and MeerKAT. We employ a machine-learning classification pipeline (MUWCLASS) for the first time in such a crowded stellar environment and introduce the globular-cluster-specific training dataset, which compiles previously classified sources from 25 Galactic globular clusters. Given the challenges presented by the high-density environment, we compare the MUWCLASS results with traditional classification methods. We confirm counterpart associations suggested in previous works and also identify new confident and candidate associations. We discuss the implications of our classification results in light of models of cluster dynamical evolution and binary formation/destruction.
